# The Examination.

On this account, those who blaspheme against the Son of God and die hating him would be saved just as well as those who die blessing him. This position maintains that one can go to heaven by two contrary paths, even by the path that leads to hell. 

2. Do the Jews believe that the Messiah <note>original: "Messias"</note>, whom they look for, shall offer himself as a sacrifice for their sins? And if they do not believe in that sacrifice, is there any pardon for them? 

3. Do they believe that the Messiah, whom they expect, shall be the same Person and the same Man who has already come once, and whose death they brought about <note>original: "procured"</note>? If they believe it, will they cause him to die again another time? If they do not believe in him (as indeed we know they do not), does the difference really lie only in a "circumstance of time"? 

4. They not only do not wait for the same Messiah, the same Person, but they are fashioning <note>original: "forging"</note> for themselves another quite contrary figure who, instead of dying for others, shall cause millions of men to die and will fill the world with blood and slaughter. Do two Messiahs so contrary to one another differ only in a "circumstance"? 

5. There are some circumstances which belong to the essence <note>In philosophy and theology, "essence" refers to the fundamental nature of a thing; the author argues that the identity of the Messiah is not a minor detail (circumstance) but a fundamental truth (essence).</note> of a

<summary>The author argues against the idea that Jewish and Christian beliefs about the Messiah differ only in timing, claiming instead that the Jewish conception of a conquering Messiah is fundamentally contrary to the Christian view of a sacrificial Savior.</summary>
<keywords>Messiah, Messias, Jewish theology, Christian theology, salvation, sacrifice, blasphemy, essence vs. circumstance</keywords>

--- Page 288 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument regarding whether the timing of the Messiah’s arrival is a minor detail or a fundamental matter of faith. It continues from the previous page, which stated that some circumstances belong to the "essence of a..."</meta>

matter of faith. There have been some heretics <note>original: "Hereticks"; individuals whose religious opinions were at odds with established church doctrine</note> who claimed that the resurrection of the dead had already happened and that, consequently, we should no longer expect it in the future <margin>2 Timothy 2:17</margin> <note>The original text cites 1 Tim 2:17, but the reference to the "resurrection being past" is found in 2 Timothy 2:17–18 regarding Hymenaeus and Philetus.</note>. Could they be cleared of the charge of blasphemy simply by saying the difference was merely a "circumstance of time"—that they had only substituted the past tense for the future? Can common sense agree to that?

Let us observe here that while the prophets sometimes express the future by using the past tense, they never express the past by using the future tense. When they said, "*For unto us a child is born*" <note>A reference to Isaiah 9:6. This is an example of the "prophetic perfect" tense, where a future event is described in the past tense to emphasize its certainty.</note>, they spoke of what was still to come as if it had already arrived, to signal that the prediction was as certain as if it had already occurred. 

But when they spoke of a thing that had already passed, they never represented it as something yet to come; for what purpose would they do so? The future eventually comes to pass, but the past never becomes the future again. Time moves forward but never backward. Furthermore, those prophecies that represent the future are turned into history when they are fulfilled, but history never turns back into prophecy.

->*T H E   E N D*<-
<note>original: "FINIS."</note>

<summary>The author concludes his argument by stating that the distinction between past and future is essential to faith; he uses the logic of biblical prophecy to show that while the future can be spoken of as certain, the past cannot be treated as a future expectation.</summary>
<keywords>faith, heretics, resurrection, prophecy, history, time, biblical interpretation, Messiah</keywords>

He has caused the Church to find a mountain to rest on, just as the floating Ark did at last. Or, like the disciples of Christ—who were the true Church—being on the sea and tossed by the tempest; but when Christ, their beloved Master, came to them, then all was made calm again. So it has pleased God that not only the Church in general has felt the effects of His goodness, but also, in particular, this French Church of Westminster,

# On the Times that Preceded the *Days of Christ*, and How They Meet in Him. His Genealogy and Other Mysteries, Preparatory to His *First Coming*.

---

### *Six days for the Creation finished in Adam. Six periods, each consisting of 660 years, from the creation of the first Adam to the death of the second <note>original: "Second," referring to Jesus Christ as the 'Second Adam' (1 Corinthians 15:45)</note>. The correspondence of the six periods with the six days of the Creation.*

From the Creation to the death of *Christ*, we calculate 3,960 years, which is six times 660. And just as the first *Adam*, the final work of the Creation, was formed on the sixth

<summary>This page introduces a chronological framework for biblical history, proposing that the 3,960 years from Creation to the death of Christ are divided into six distinct periods of 660 years each, mirroring the six days of Creation.</summary>
<keywords>Biblical chronology, Adam, Jesus Christ, Creation, 660-year periods, Second Adam, typology</keywords>

--- Page 298 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion of the six periods of history, beginning with the formation of the first Adam on the sixth day of Creation.</meta>

sixth day, so the Second *Adam* <note>original: "Adam," referring to Jesus Christ (1 Corinthians 15:45)</note> was produced about the latter end of the sixth period of 660 years. Such are the measures and divisions of time which ran from the Creation to the accomplishment of our redemption.

That which now presents itself is worthy to be attentively considered. Whenever God made any general and miraculous change contrary to the natural order of the Creation, every one of those changes happened within one of the six periods, corresponding to one of the six days of Creation, specifically the day to which that change is an exception. The examples will clarify this.

Four great changes have happened in the world: one on Earth, two in Heaven, and one in the production of Man. On Earth, this occurred when it was wholly covered by the Flood. In Heaven, it occurred when the Sun stayed <note>original: "staid," a reference to Joshua 10:12–13, where the sun stood still in the sky</note>, and later when it went backward <note>A reference to 2 Kings 20:11, the sign of the sundial given to King Hezekiah</note>. In the production of Man, it occurred when a Virgin gave birth to a child. For although Christ alone has had such a birth, yet he ought to be considered not just as an individual man, but as being the second *Adam*, the counterpart to the former Adam in several respects.

All other changes that happened in nature (such as the parting of the waters in the *Red Sea*, the reversing of the *Jordan River*, the speaking of *Balaam's donkey*, and other wonders) were only specific occurrences. Likewise, we

I. On the third day of the Creation, God caused the waters that covered the whole earth to withdraw and made the dry land appear. To the contrary, within the third period <note>The author uses "period" to refer to a 660-year epoch, as established on the previous page.</note> of the years of the world, God covered the whole earth with waters by the Flood.

III. On the fifth day of the Creation, the Sun began to be seen; he had already lighted the whole preceding day, which was the fourth; but there was then no eye, as yet, to see his light. The first period ends at the year of the world 660. <note>The author uses "year of the world" (Anno Mundi) to count years starting from the biblical Creation.</note>

The third period ran from the year 1320 to the year 1980. Within that interval falls the year of the Creation 1566, when the Flood happened.

The fourth period ran from the year 1980 to the year 2640. Within that interval falls the year of the Creation 2555, when the miraculous solstice <note>Here, "solstice" refers to the sun standing still in the sky, rather than the seasonal event.</note> happened in the time of *Joshua*.

If the world was created in the springtime, as is very likely, this term of 3,960 years would expire in the same season. And these six periods, like the six days of the Creation, were followed by Christ's eternal rest, whose Sabbath is not limited by the evening and the morning, unlike the Sabbath of the Creation.

### Concerning the name Enosh, misspelled in several versions, both Latin and French.

The Hebrew name of that grandson of *Adam*, and one of Christ's ancestors, is *Enosh*. The Greek language <note>original: "Tongue"</note>, which is the original language of the New Testament, lacks an aspirated sound <note>In this context, the "sh" sound.</note> for the end of that word and says only *Enos*; but the translators

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of biblical translations, specifically focusing on the phonetic transcription of the name Enosh and a perceived error in the genealogy of Christ found in the Gospel of Luke compared to the books of Moses.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>

translators who write *Henos* instead of *Enos* are without excuse. The word *Enos*, or more accurately *Enosh*, in Hebrew signifies a sad or mournful man. Why that name was given to that Patriarch is a question in itself. However, the aspiration <note>The addition of an "h" sound at the beginning of the word.</note> added by various writers at the beginning of that word removes its meaning and leaves it with none at all. Since all the names of those ancient Patriarchs are meaningful and point to some event in the history of their time, we should not destroy <note>original: "annihilate"</note> those meanings.

### *A dangerous note in one of the French editions of the New Testament regarding Luke 3:36.*

Those annotators say this: "*Moses only mentions one Cainan, from which it appears that he has not listed all those who followed Enosh.*"

They should instead have said, "*Moses mentions only one Cainan, or Kenan; therefore, that second Cainan, inserted by several writers in this passage of St. Luke, is wrongly assumed to exist.*" This is also the opinion of the best interpreters.

To justify the carelessness <note>original: "inadvertency"</note> of the copyists, shall we claim that Moses's account is flawed <note>original: "defective"</note>?

### *The first millennium <note>original: "Millenary"</note> of the years of the world ended seventy years after the death of the first Adam. The fourth millennium was completed seventy years, or a little more, after the birth <note>original: "Nativity"</note> of the second Adam <note>The "second Adam" is a theological term for Jesus Christ.</note>.*

The first man died in the 930th year of his life, which was also the year 930 of the world's history; therefore, the year 1,000 of the world occurred seventy years after the death of *Adam*. But many chronologists <note>Scholars who study the timeline of historical events.</note> calculate seventy years from the birth of *Christ* to the destruction <note>original: "ruine"</note> of the second Temple <note>The Jewish Temple in Jerusalem, destroyed by the Romans in 70 AD.</note>, which we believe happened in the year 4,000 of the world. From this, it would follow that there were seventy years between *Christ's* birth and the end of the fourth millennium; nevertheless

### *A Remarkable Proposition and the Inclusion of the Years of Christ, From His Birth to His Death, Within the Years That Passed From the Birth of David to the Death of Christ.*

The days of Christ from His birth to His death were thirty-two years and six months. The years that passed from the birth of David to the death of Christ were thirty-two times thirty-two years and six months. The proof is easy for anyone who knows even a little arithmetic. We place the birth of Christ around the Autumnal Equinox <note>The fall equinox, typically occurring in late September.</note> of the Year of the World 3928 <term>Year of the World: Also known as Anno Mundi (AM), a calendar era based on the biblical account of the creation of the world.</term>, and His death happened around the Spring Equinox in the year 3960. This totals thirty-two years and a half. 

As for David, he was born in the Year of the World 2920. From that year to the year of the death of Christ, there were 1,040 years. These 1,040 years make 32 times 32 years and six months. That is to say: 32 times 32 years, plus 32 times six months—a number multiplied by itself. The years of Christ ending at His death, multiplied by their own number, by going back, do therefore

<summary>The author presents a mathematical proof linking the 32.5-year lifespan of Jesus to the 1,040 years between David's birth and Christ's death, noting that 1,040 is exactly 32 times 32.5.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus Christ, King David, Chronology, Arithmetic, Anno Mundi, Autumnal Equinox, Spring Equinox, Biblical math</keywords>

--- Page 311 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical comparison between the lifespan of Jesus Christ and the timeline of King David, then transitions into a discussion of King Hezekiah's life extension.</meta>

therefore match that of the birth <note>original: "nativity"</note> of *David*. We know that when the Prophets speak of *Christ*, they often call Him *David* in many respects; and it is observed that the reign of *David* in *Jerusalem* lasted thirty-two years and six months.

### Concerning the fifteen years added to the life of Hezekiah.

In our observations on the Ten Commandments <note>original: "Decalogue"</note>, we touched upon a reason for that number of fifteen years; here is another consideration we bring to the subject.

That extension of life <note>original: "prolongation"</note> was granted to *Hezekiah* so that he might see a son of his reach an age where he could reign. At that time, however, he had no son. But in the interval of fifteen years, he could not only father a successor but also see him reach the age to rule <note>original: "sway the Scepter"</note>; and that was the thing *Hezekiah* desired to see before his death.

Yet it should be observed regarding those fifteen years that the first two passed before *Hezekiah* had a son; he did not have him until the third year. This son was *Manasseh*, who was only twelve years old when his father *Hezekiah* reached the end of the final fifteen years of his life. To test the faith of *Hezekiah*, God kept him in suspense until the third year;

<summary>This page concludes the chronological link between David and Christ's lifespans and explains that Hezekiah's life was extended by fifteen years specifically so he could produce an heir, Manasseh, and see him reach the age of twelve.</summary>
<keywords>King Hezekiah, King David, Jesus Christ, Manasseh, Biblical chronology, Decalogue, succession, life extension</keywords>

year; just as after God had granted him his life, he appointed the third day for him to go up to the House of the Lord.

### *Concerning the fifteen stairs by which men ascended, or went up to the Temple, corresponding to the fifteen years added to the life of Hezekiah.*

The hypothesis <note>original: "Hypothese"</note> or supposition we use here is not actually found in the Sacred History <note>meaning the Bible</note>—specifically, the idea that there were fifteen stairs around the Temple for ascending into it. However, we should not reject all historical records <note>original: "Monuments"</note> that appear to be well-constructed <note>original: "well framed Characters"</note>. The same author who counts those fifteen stairs also applies the fifteen Psalms to them called *Graduals* <note>original: "Graduals"; these are the "Songs of Ascents," Psalms 120–134</note>—namely, the 120th Psalm and the fourteen following it—which were sung while going up to the Temple, one on each stair.

Even if we take this as merely human history, it is still very likely. And assuming it to be true, a wonderful analogy <note>a symbolic comparison</note> can be found. God promised fifteen years of life to Hezekiah, and as a pledge <note>original: "earnest"</note>

<summary>This page explores the symbolic connection between the fifteen years added to King Hezekiah's life and the tradition of fifteen stairs leading to the Temple, upon which the fifteen "Gradual" Psalms were sung.</summary>
<keywords>King Hezekiah, Temple of Jerusalem, Gradual Psalms, Biblical analogy, Psalms 120-134, Sacred History, architecture</keywords>

--- Page 313 ---
<meta>This page continues the symbolic link between King Hezekiah’s fifteen additional years of life and the architecture of the Temple, specifically focusing on a mathematical calculation involving the number fifteen.</meta>

of his promise, He tells him, *"Upon the third day you shall go up to the Temple,"* to which men ascended by fifteen stairs. Therefore, *Hezekiah* had a promise to live as many years as the steps he would ascend to enter the Temple: fifteen years for fifteen steps.

---

### The last fifteen years of Hezekiah, multiplied by their own number, reach the year that followed the finishing of the Second Temple.

Hezekiah began the fifteen years that were added to his life in the year of the world <note>original: "year of the world"; Latin: Anno Mundi, a calendar system counting from the biblical creation</note> 3295. The building of the Second Temple was finished in the year 3519, and in the following year, which was 3520, the construction was completed <note>original: "the building ceased"</note>. The time that passed from the year 3295 to the year 3520 totals 225 years, which equals fifteen times fifteen years. Within that interval, the ruin of the First Temple occurred. But at the end of the cycle <note>original: "revolution"</note> of fifteen times fifteen years, the Second Temple was finished.

<summary>This page presents a chronological calculation linking the fifteen years added to Hezekiah's life to the completion of the Second Temple, noting that the 225-year interval between these events is exactly fifteen times fifteen.</summary>
<keywords>King Hezekiah, Second Temple, First Temple, Biblical Chronology, Anno Mundi, numerology, 225 years, Temple architecture</keywords>

### Why, among the ancestors of Christ who had brothers, did he descend from the youngest rather than from the eldest?

Why did he come from *Seth* rather than from *Abel*, who preceded *Seth*? From *Shem* <note>original: "Sem"</note>, rather than from his eldest brother *Japheth*? From *Abraham*, rather than from his brother *Nahor*? From *Jacob*, rather than from *Esau*? From *Judah* <note>original: "Juda"</note>, rather than from his three brothers born before him? From *David*, who was only the seventh son of *Jesse*? From *Nathan*, *Solomon's* younger brother? From *Rhesa*, *Zerubbabel's* <note>original: "Zorobabel's"</note> second son, rather than from *Abiud* the eldest? From that eldest son, *Joseph* (the Virgin *Mary's* husband) descended; but the *Virgin*, from whom *Christ* was born, descended from *Rhesa*, who was only the youngest brother.

An example to the contrary may be offered. Of the twins that *Judah* had by *Tamar* <note>original: "Thamar"</note>, *Perez* <note>original: "Pharez"</note> was the first who came out of their mother's womb, and *Christ* came from him; but the holy history recounts that this birth was unusual <note>original: "monstrous"; in this context, it refers to a deviation from the natural or expected order of birth, as Perez "broke through" before his brother Zerah</note>,

<summary>This page questions why the lineage of Jesus Christ often bypassed the firstborn sons in favor of younger brothers, citing numerous biblical examples from Seth to the family of Zerubbabel. It notes Perez as a rare exception whose birth was considered a supernatural occurrence.</summary>
<keywords>Ancestors of Christ, genealogy, primogeniture, Seth, Shem, Abraham, Jacob, Judah, David, Nathan, Zerubbabel, Perez, Virgin Mary, biblical lineage</keywords>

--- Page 315 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the biblical genealogy of Christ, specifically addressing why younger brothers were often chosen over the firstborn in his lineage, finishing the account of the birth of Zerah and Perez.</meta>

the hand of *Zerah* <note>original: "Zarah"</note> appeared before the birth of his brother *Perez* <note>original: "Pharez"</note>. Because this happened in a way that was contrary to nature, it could not naturally give him the status of the firstborn <term>primogeniture: the right of the firstborn child to inherit the family estate or title</term> or seniority.

Now, as to why God—when bestowing the honor of having Christ descend from a specific family or lineage—has chosen and preferred the youngest brothers, this one reason will suffice: It is well known how much the right of the firstborn has always been valued, and what advantages and privileges even the Law of *Israel* assigned to it. Yet sometimes, by a command from Heaven, the rights that were usually tied to the firstborn have been transferred to the youngest. This is seen in the example of *Esau* and *Jacob*, of whom it was said that the eldest should serve the youngest. Just as God intended to show that grace does not depend on age, He has also demonstrated that the superiority <note>original: "preheminency"</note> of Christ did not come from any firstborn status of His ancestors, since He descended from those who were merely the youngest sons.

<summary>This page explains that God often chose younger brothers for Christ's lineage to demonstrate that divine grace is not tied to birth order or traditional rights of the firstborn. It uses the birth of Zerah and Perez, and the relationship between Jacob and Esau, to illustrate this spiritual principle.</summary>
<keywords>Zerah, Perez, primogeniture, firstborn rights, Jacob, Esau, lineage of Christ, divine grace, biblical genealogy</keywords>

### *Regarding all those from whom Christ descended according to the flesh, it is not recorded that any of them died a violent death.*

Among the ancestors of Christ named by St. Matthew in chapter 1, there are many—from Solomon (including him) to Jeconiah <note>original: "Jechonias"</note> (including him)—who cannot be counted as Christ's direct progenitors <term>progenitor: a direct biological ancestor</term>, but are included in his genealogy for reasons cited by biblical commentators <note>original: "Interpreters"</note>. However, regarding these men—who were all Kings of Judah—many died violent deaths; Amaziah, Joash, Amon, and Josiah all met such an end. But regarding those who were Christ's direct ancestors, we do not read of any whose lives were cut short <note>original: "prevented"</note> by a violent death. This applies to everyone before David; and David himself, though he was in many bloody battles, never received so much as a wound. Nor did his son Nathan, from whom Christ descended, nor any of Nathan's descendants, nor Zerubbabel, and so on down to Christ in the genealogy recorded in the third chapter of St. Luke.

And it cannot be argued that some of them might well have died a violent death,

### Why some of the children of Adam lived longer than he did.

It might seem that because the first man was created with a perfect temperament <note>original: "Temperament"; in this context, it refers to a perfectly balanced physical constitution or the ideal ratio of bodily "humors."</note>, his fall into sin would not have shortened his life as much as it did the lives of his children, who were mortal from the very moment of their conception. Yet, three of his descendants lived for more days than he did himself: *Noah* lived twenty years longer than Adam, *Jared* thirty-two years longer, and *Methuselah* thirty-nine years longer.

Undoubtedly, God was pleased to show people through this that they inherit death from *Adam*, but they receive life from the grace <note>original: "indulgence"</note> of God. *Adam* passed down death to *Methuselah*, but not a life of 969 years, for *Adam’s* own life was only 930 years; he could not give what he himself never possessed.

### Seven years before Christ's Nativity

The paralysis <note>original: "palsy"</note> began for the man who was cured thirty-eight years later near the Pool of *Bethesda* (John 5). Around that same time, the lame man was born who was healed at the Temple gate more than forty years later (Acts 4:22).

->*Consideration*<-

It pleased God for *Christ's* power to be revealed through the healing of long-standing <note>original: "inveterate"</note> and incurable diseases. Here are two men whose physical <note>original: "corporal"</note> disabilities preceded the birth of their Physician <note>referring to Christ</note> by seven years. Even before *Jesus Christ* was born, God prepared the circumstances <note>original: "matter and subject"</note> for him to perform miracles that would only happen much later. And regarding that lame man: although both he and his condition entered the world before the coming of *Christ*, he was not healed until after *Jesus Christ* had left the world—specifically, after his ascension into heaven.

### ...adding to that time the three days of his stay among the dead.

In agreement with all the best chronologists <note>scholars who study the timing of historical events</note>, we observe that the birth of **Christ** took place around the autumnal equinox <note>the fall equinox, occurring around September 22 or 23</note>, near the Feast of Tabernacles <note>original: "Feast of the Tabernacles"; a major Jewish autumn festival</note>. As **Christ** entered his thirtieth year, he began his ministry and carried it out for three and a half years, which ended with his death during the solemn season of Easter <note>The author uses "Easter" to refer to the period of the Passover and the Resurrection</note>. 

From this, it follows that from his birth to his death, there were thirty-two and a half years. If you add to this the time **Christ** spent in his mother's womb, it appears that from his conception to his death—or his resurrection, which comprises the entire time of his <term>Humiliation</term> <note>In theology, the "State of Humiliation" refers to the period from Christ's conception to his burial, during which he set aside his divine glory</note>—there were thirty-three years and three months. We cannot say for certain whether the three days of his stay among the dead should be included in that sum; if they should be added to it, the total would be thirty-three years, three months, and three days.

### *Six months after the birth of our Lord, a writing was produced between the Old Testament, which was ending, and the New Testament, which was beginning.*

Since Malachi <note>The last prophet of the Old Testament</note>, there had not been any writing inspired by God. The <term>dispensation</term> <note>original: "Oeconomy"; the divine system or arrangement of religious laws and history during a specific era</note> of the Old Testament was near its end, making way for the New Testament, which was to begin with the ministry of **John the Baptist**. 

The time that elapsed from the appearance of the angel in the Temple to the baptism of **John** was an interval between the two Testaments, sharing elements of both. This period extended even to the death of **Christ**, which brought the Old Testament to its conclusion shortly after the start of that interval. 

On the eighth day after the birth of **John the Baptist**, his father **Zechariah** <note>original: "Zacharias"</note> wrote down his name. Although that writing only contained the name of a man, it was of great significance, as it had been dictated by an angel in the Holy Place and identified the forerunner of the **Messiah**. That writing

Writing might be styled the last of the Old Testament and the first of the New Testament; or indeed, it happened between both, and it seemed to signify that as the first Testament had been given in writing, so should the second also. The pens of the Prophets having been still for over 300 years, men might have imagined that henceforth no more writings composed <note>original: "indited"</note> by the Holy Spirit <note>original: "holy Ghost"</note> would be produced. But that writing of **Zechariah** <note>original: "Zacharias"</note> was like a prelude to the Scriptures of the New Testament, which followed afterward.

# GENERALITIES.

### *Regarding the measurement of the time Christ spent on earth.*

I. The total sum of the years **Christ** lived in this world is never stated as a single number in Scripture; it is only found there in separate words. The whole number of the years of the **Patriarchs** <note>The biblical fathers of the human race or the Hebrew people, such as Abraham or Isaac</note> and other persons is found in the holy history: the 930 years of **Adam**, the 365 of **Enoch** <note>original: "Henoc"</note>, and so on. Why are those of **Christ** not included? Because they have no end, and therefore cannot be numbered. We read indeed of part of his

<summary>This page argues that Zechariah’s writing of John the Baptist’s name served as a prelude to the New Testament and begins an inquiry into why Christ’s total years on earth are not explicitly numbered in the Bible.</summary>
<keywords>Zechariah, Holy Spirit, New Testament, Christ, Patriarchs, Adam, Enoch, chronology</keywords>

--- Page 329 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on why the Bible does not explicitly state the total number of years Christ lived on Earth, contrasting this with the detailed genealogies of the Old Testament Patriarchs.</meta>

his years, but who shall declare his duration? *Isaiah 53:8.* The years of his sojourning on earth ought to be numbered together with those of his residence in heaven. This is unique <note>original: "particular"</note> to Christ: death has not interrupted his eternity.

II. For other causes. The history of the New Testament never gives the total sum of the years of any one person. The daughter of **Jairus** has her age noted at the time she died, but she was raised from the dead, and it is not recorded how long she lived afterward. Furthermore, there are some very ancient persons mentioned, such as **Zechariah**, **Elizabeth**, and **Anna** <note>original: "Anne"</note> the prophetess; but the history does not express how long they lived in total. The total number of the years of the ancient Fathers was observed so that it might serve the chronology that extended to the coming of Christ; the times included in that chronology were first reckoned by the ages <note>original: "Angels" — likely a typographical error for "Ages" in the original text, referring to the generations of the Patriarchs</note> of the Fathers, and afterward by the measures of other significant <note>original: "considerable"</note> periods. But once **Christ** had come, there was no necessity to learn how many years his contemporaries, or those that came after, lived.

III. Although the Scripture names not the whole sum of the time that passed from **Christ's** birth to his death, yet it is lawful to inquire into it; indeed, the holy history invites us to do so, since it does mark the

<summary>This page explains that Christ's total lifespan is not summed up in Scripture because his existence is eternal, and notes that New Testament chronology focuses on the arrival of Christ rather than the lifespans of individuals.</summary>
<keywords>chronology, Christ, New Testament, Jairus's daughter, Zechariah, Elizabeth, Anna the Prophetess, Patriarchs, eternity</keywords>

the specific <note>original: "particular"</note> measures that make up the total of that sum. Very mysterious connections <note>original: "agreements"</note> are found within them, as we have seen in the list of the years that passed from the birth of **David** to the death of **Christ**. Here is another correspondence that should not be overlooked: 390 years passed from the revolt of the **Ten Tribes** under **Jeroboam** to the destruction of **Jerusalem** and the first Temple. During that interval, God’s patience endured the sins <note>original: "iniquities"</note> of **Israel** and **Judah**. The Prophet **Ezekiel** was commanded (**Ezekiel 4:4, 5, 6**) to represent that patience through a symbolic act <note>original: "Type"</note> of 390 days, during which **Ezekiel** would bear the sins of **Israel** and **Judah**; the number of days corresponded to the number of years. 

The number of months that passed from **Christ's** Nativity <note>the birth of Jesus</note> to his death is likewise the same; for the thirty-two and a half years between the two events add up to 390 months. Throughout that entire time, **Christ** bore the sins of his people until he made atonement <note>original: "expiated"</note> for them by his death. During those 390 months, he bore not only the sins of those 390 years, but also the sins of 3,900 years and more, counting from the time sin first entered the world. Indeed, he also bore the sins that have happened since then, and those that are yet to happen.

<summary>This page identifies a symbolic parallel between the 390 years of Israel's sin and the 390 months of Christ's life, suggesting that Christ's time on Earth was a deliberate fulfillment of biblical prophecy and a period of bearing human sin.</summary>
<keywords>Ezekiel, Jeroboam, Jerusalem, chronology, 390 years, 390 months, atonement, Ten Tribes, biblical numerology</keywords>

### Why the Angel did not carry Jesus Christ into Egypt, but gave that charge to Joseph?

When the Devil was tempting Christ, he could argue <note>original: "alledge"</note> to him, *The Angels in their hands shall bear you up.* If it were ever necessary <note>original: "needful"</note> for them to perform that service <note>original: "do him that Office"</note> for him, it was on that occasion. And yet, that Angel, instead of carrying *Jesus*, is content to give that duty <note>original: "Commiffion"</note> to *Joseph*, warning him of the danger the infant was in. 

But it is known that where ordinary means can serve, God is not accustomed <note>original: "wont"</note> to make use of extraordinary ways. He has divided those duties <note>original: "Offices"</note> between that Angel and *Joseph*. *Joseph* could not look into <note>original: "dive into"</note> *Herod's* private chamber <note>original: "clofet" – a private room or study</note> to know the plotting of that tyrant; therefore, an Angel is sent to reveal <note>original: "difcover"</note> that secret to him.

### Christ's Residence in Galilee; and Why Much Later He Spoke of the Sign of Jonah?

From the time that *Jesus* was brought to *Nazareth*, a town in *Galilee*, his usual home was always in that province. The reasons for this are well known; but the ignorant people <note>original: "the ignorants"</note> inferred from this that he was a *Galilean* by birth. Because of that, they would not acknowledge him as a prophet, for they held a prejudice among themselves—though it was false and insulting <note>original: "abusive"</note>—that *no prophet was to come out of Galilee*. 

But Christ declared to them that they should be convinced by the sign of a *Galilean* prophet, namely <note>original: "viz." – abbreviation for the Latin *videlicet*</note> *Jonah*. For that prophet was a native of *Gath-Hepher*, a town belonging to the tribe of *Zebulun*, and *Zebulun* was located in *Galilee* (2 *Kings* 14:25; *Joshua* 19:13; *Isaiah* 9:1).

# The twelfth year of Christ.

### Concerning the eleventh year of Solomon's reign, and the completion of the eleventh year since the Wise Men from the East announced the birth of the King of the Jews.

Several scholars <note>original: "Interpreters"</note>, based on strong evidence <note>original: "reasons"</note>, claim that the arrival of the Wise Men who worshipped *Christ* in *Bethlehem* did not happen immediately after his birth. Instead, they arrived during his second year—either at the beginning or toward the middle of it. If this is true, it follows that the debate which *Christ* had in the Temple with the teachers <note>original: "Doctors," referring to Doctors of the Law or religious scholars</note> occurred in the eleventh year after the Wise Men first announced him as King.

The first Temple was completed in the eleventh year after *Solomon* was proclaimed King in *Jerusalem*. Similarly, *Christ*—who is greater than *Solomon*—began to teach in the Temple in the eleventh year after the Wise Men proclaimed him King in *Jerusalem*.

### The first words of Christ that are recorded to have been spoken in the Temple.

We have seen that the message of the angel to Zechariah <note>original: "Zacharias"</note> was the first word that God sent regarding the beginning of the New Testament, and it was delivered in the Temple itself. # *The Ax is laid to the root of the Trees*
->Matthew 3:10<-

Despite the opinions of several interpreters—who claim the "ax" signifies the instrument of vengeance God was about to pour upon the Jews—the Romans were actually that instrument. They already had their foot at the root, ruling even in Jerusalem, and they eventually cut off that state entirely. This is how the best commentators <note>original: "Expositors"</note> understand the passage.

But it remains to be asked why the Romans are represented by an ax. Let us finally offer our own theory <note>original: "conjecture"</note>. The symbol of high-ranking Roman officials was an ax that was carried before them; more or fewer were carried depending on the rank of their office <note>The author is referring to the *fasces*, a bundle of rods containing an ax that symbolized a Roman magistrate's power to punish and execute</note>. Perhaps the ax mentioned here by John the Baptist alludes to that symbol of Roman power, indicating that the Jewish state would be destroyed by it.

***

# *The unquenchable fire*
->Matthew 3:12<- 
<note>original: "Matth. 12. 3." — The original text likely contains a printing error, as the "unquenchable fire" is described in Matthew 3:12.</note>

That fire does not always refer to the fires of Hell. The word often signifies a vengeance that torments and consumes until the very end. Such was the vengeance that God poured upon the Jews during the final destruction <note>original: "ruine"</note> of Jerusalem, which John the Baptist predicts here.

->Indeed,<-

Yes, it is possible that the "unquenchable fire" he mentions should be understood literally. The Temple, in which the Jews placed their greatest confidence, was completely burned down. The Roman General <note>Titus, who led the Roman forces during the Siege of Jerusalem in 70 AD</note> wanted to save that building; however, after his men set it on fire, he tried to have it extinguished, but it was completely impossible to prevent that massive structure from being entirely destroyed. many Jews who had fled into the Temple also died in those flames.

### The 30th year of Christ and the first of his ministry

This was not his thirtieth year completed, but only the beginning of it (see Luke 3:23). 
From that time until Christ's death, three and a half years passed <note>original: "a week and a half of years," referring to the prophetic "weeks" in the Book of Daniel</note>, totaling three and a half years—the duration of his ministry (see Daniel 9:27). 
Christ's thirtieth year began with his baptism, his temptation in the wilderness, and his forty-day fast.

# CONSIDERATIONS.

### *A comparison of the thirtieth year of Christ, with the thirty years since the Promise made to Abraham.*

That famous Promise, which stated *that in Abraham all the families of the earth should be blessed*, cannot be understood as anything other than the blessing of *Christ*; this is beyond controversy. But from the time of that Promise until *Isaac* was declared the true son and heir of *Abraham*, there was a span of thirty years. Likewise, from the birth <note>original: "Nativity"</note> of *Christ* until he was solemnly declared to be the Son of God by the voice from heaven—which happened at his Baptism—thirty years passed.

There is yet another parallel. The claims <note>original: "pretensions"</note> of *Ishmael*, who sought the succession of *Abraham*, were declared unjust and unfounded, and *Isaac* was declared the only heir. Likewise, before the Baptism of *Christ*, it was said to those who were not the children of *Abraham* except by physical descent <note>original: "according to the flesh"</note>—being no more his true heirs than *Ishmael* was—*Do not think to say to yourselves, "We have Abraham* <meta>The sentence concludes on the next page with "as our father."</meta>

<summary>This page draws a typological parallel between the thirty years from God's promise to Abraham until Isaac's recognition as heir, and the thirty years from Christ's birth to his baptismal recognition as the Son of God.</summary>
<keywords>Abraham, Christ, Isaac, Ishmael, Promise, Baptism, Nativity, biblical parallels, typology, heirship</keywords>

### The Season of Christ's forty days in the Wilderness.

The season of *Lent*, which is observed by many Christians, does not coincide <note>original: "agrees not to"</note> with the timing of the miraculous fast of *Christ*, whom they claim <note>original: "pretend"</note> to imitate; these forty days are observed immediately before *Easter*. However, a considerable amount of time passed from the fortieth day of *Christ's* stay in the *Wilderness* until the next following *Passover*, as is clearly shown by the deeds and circumstances recorded by the *Evangelists*. 

After *Christ* had ended his fast of forty days, he returned to *John the Baptist*, who then showed him to his listeners and told them, "*Behold the Lamb of God*"; consequently, some of his disciples followed *Christ*. From there, *Christ* traveled toward *Galilee* (John 1:15, etc.). Three days later, the miracle of *Cana* occurred. Afterward, *Christ* went to *Capernaum*. All these events happened before the *Easter* <note>The author uses "Easter" here to refer to the Passover festival.</note> that followed the forty days of *Christ's* stay in the *Wilderness* (John 2:13).

<summary>This page argues that the liturgical observation of Lent is chronologically inaccurate, noting that several biblical events occurred between Christ's fast in the wilderness and the subsequent Passover, unlike the modern church calendar.</summary>

### Satan, when he presented himself to Christ, claimed a specific identity.

It is very likely, and it is the opinion of several interpreters, that he feigned <note>original: "feigned himself"—pretended or disguised himself</note> to be a good angel. Let us add that he likely claimed to be not only a good angel, but one of those angels to whom God gives the commission to rule over kingdoms (Daniel 10:13, 20 and 11:1). 

In that role <note>original: "quality"—the character or capacity in which he appeared</note> which Satan took upon himself, he offered the kingdoms of the world to *Christ*. Long before this, all the kingdoms of the earth had been promised to the Son of God (Psalm 2:8, etc.). Satan tried to persuade him that he was the one who would bring that promise to pass, claiming that he had received power over all kingdoms and the authority to dispose of them as he saw fit. The Evangelists <note>The authors of the four Gospels: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John.</note>, in few words, observe the cunning depths of that adversary.

### No man was ever carried by the angels: What then do the words of the Psalmist signify, "They shall bear you up in their hands"?

The angel that appeared to Philip (Acts 8:26, 39, 40) and gave him the order to go toward the official <note>original: "Eunuch"—a high-ranking court officer from Ethiopia</note>, who was quite far from him, did not carry him there. Indeed, we do not find that the angels were ever employed in that kind of service for men. preferred before those of *Moses*, or any other.

The opening of the *Red Sea*, the miraculous solstice <term>solstice: here referring to the biblical account of the sun standing still in the sky</term> that happened at *Joshua's* request, and several other wonders of the *Old Testament* have indeed shone more brightly and have been fuller of majesty than most of *Christ's* miracles. 

But *Christ's* miracles, as he had come for the salvation of humanity, have resulted in greater benefit <note>original: "redounded to mans profit"</note> for mankind. Because the human body is the noblest of all visible creatures—for whose sake all others were made—it has been the most frequent object of *Christ's* miracles. To give sight to a man born blind was an act of greater kindness <note>original: "beneficence"</note> than stopping the sun. Furthermore, neither *Moses* nor *Joshua* ever raised any person from the dead; and the *New Testament* provides more examples of that wonder than we can find in the *Old Testament*.

### *A progression of Christ's miracles regarding the bodies of men.*

Before Christ raised anyone from the dead, he healed many of the living; and before he healed diseased people, he provided nourishment to those who were healthy <note>original: "whole"</note> when he turned water into wine. He began his miracles in such a way that he moved from providing for the healthy to healing the sick, and finally proceeded to raising the dead. We touched on that progression <note>original: "Gradation"</note> in our observations on the Creed <note>original: "the Symbole," likely referring to the Apostles' Creed, often called the *Symbolum*</note>, but there is more to it. For after he had acted upon people's bodies, he finally displayed his riches even further—especially after his Resurrection—namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, upon people's souls, by pouring <note>original: "shedding"</note> into them the extraordinary gifts of the Holy Spirit.

### *The time that passed from when Christ began to foretell his Death and Resurrection until their fulfillment, John 2:19, 20, 21, 22.*

The Temple he was speaking of was his own body. He said, "*In three days, I will raise it up*." Those three days were completed exactly three years after that prediction. For the prophecy was spoken at the first Feast of the Passover following the baptism of Christ. From that Passover until the fourth one—when Christ died and rose on the third day—precisely three full years had passed. During the first Passover of Christ’s ministry, he began to foretell his death and resurrection, and on the final Passover, he fulfilled that prophecy.

The three years that passed from one event to the other even seem to be included within the literal meaning of the "three days," 

<summary>This page explains Christ's prophecy that he would "raise the temple" in three days, interpreting it as his own body. It notes the symbolic and literal timing, highlighting that his ministry lasted exactly three years from this first prediction to its fulfillment.</summary>

<keywords>Jesus Christ, Temple of Jerusalem, Resurrection prophecy, Passover, Chronology of Christ's ministry, John 2:19-22, Oeconomy</keywords>

--- Page 363 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the chronological connection between the "three days" mentioned in Christ's prophecy of his Resurrection and the three-year duration of his ministry, referencing biblical precedents for substituting days for years.</meta>

...that are marked for *Christ's* Resurrection.

It is known that sometimes three days are interpreted as three years (*Amos* 4:4), and sometimes a specific number of years corresponds to a specific number of days. For example, regarding the forty days that the spies spent surveying the land of *Canaan*, God ordered that the *Israelites* should spend forty years in the wilderness (*Numbers* 14:34). The literal and natural meaning of the "three days" mentioned by *Christ* does not prevent them from also referring to the three years that were to immediately precede or conclude with those three days. In this way, he not only foretold how many days he would remain in the state of the dead, but also—though implicitly—exactly how much time would pass from the moment of that prophecy until its fulfillment: a precise measure of three years.

### A digression against certain people who claim to have the power of healing a sick body merely by the touch of their hand.

I would not deign to mention that imposture <note>original: "Imposture"; a deceptive trick or fraud</note>, much less to refute it, if it had not found belief even among common people <note>original: "vulgar spirits"</note>. A lord of very high rank, who had chosen me for his spiritual guidance <note>original: "direction of his conscience"</note>, was afflicted with a long and serious illness. He forgot himself so far as to send—without my knowledge—for one of those wicked people called **Strokers** <term>Strokers: practitioners of "royal touch" or folk medicine who claimed to cure diseases like scrofula by stroking the patient with their hands</term>. But what happened soon after was the death of that poor lord, who nevertheless had enough time to confess his fault to me and ask God's forgiveness, which he did in my presence, showing great remorse for it.

Now, the boasting of these impostors is grounded on a principle that many believe: that a **seventh son**, whose birth was not preceded by the birth of any sister, has the power to heal by touching and stroking with his hand alone <note>The "seventh son of a seventh son" is a common folk belief in British and Irish mythology, attributing supernatural healing powers or second sight to such individuals</note>. ### The first person sick with paralysis <note>original: "Palsey"</note> our Lord cured, Mark 2:1, 15. Why, among so many diseased persons whom the Son of God healed, he spoke of none of their sins except to the man sick with paralysis.

Christ never mentioned any causes for diseases other than sin, because they could not be taken away except by him. And yet, although all diseases come from sin, *Christ* never expressed that cause except in cases of paralysis <note>original: "Palsey"</note>. To the man who was the first to be cured of it, he said, "*Your sins are forgiven*." To another, whose story <note>original: "History"</note> we shall hear shortly, he said, "*Sin no more, lest a worse thing come to you*"; but he never spoke this way to those who were afflicted with any other kind of disease, such as those with a fever, dropsy <note>term: dropsy; an old term for edema, or swelling caused by excess fluid</note>, wasting fevers <note>original: "Hecticks"; likely referring to the persistent fever associated with tuberculosis</note>, leprosy, blindness, or any other bodily defect. Indeed, when he was asked concerning the man who was born blind, whether that affliction came upon him because of some sin of his own, or for the sins of his father or mother, his answer was that there was no such cause.

<summary>The author notes that while all illness is rooted in sin, Christ only explicitly linked sin to the disease of paralysis (palsy) during his healing miracles, unlike his treatment of blindness or fever.</summary>

<keywords>paralysis, palsy, sin, Mark 2, Christ's miracles, dropsy, hectic fever, divine healing</keywords>

--- Page 371 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into why Christ specifically addressed the sins of those suffering from paralysis, suggesting that the disease serves as a physical restraint against sinful actions.</meta>

<page-num>65</page-num>

Now, as to why those sick with paralysis <note>original: "Palsey"</note> were the only ones among the many patients he healed to whom he spoke of their sins, the reason is very uncertain. Yet, let us be permitted to suggest <note>original: "alleage"</note> this: among the bodily illnesses that last a long time, paralysis stops the progress of many sins regarding outward actions—sins that a person might commit if they had full use of their senses and the ability to move freely. The paralysis that keeps a person bound restrains many effects of the soul's corruption. If it happens that his body recovers its freedom, he ought to choose voluntarily to avoid those sins which he was previously forced to refrain from. But paralysis—which was his "rod" <note>The "rod" refers to a tool of divine discipline or correction.</note>—is the disease that stops more sins, and for a longer duration, than any other bodily ailment <note>original: "indisposition"</note>.

<summary>The author proposes that Christ specifically addressed sin when healing paralysis because the disease physically prevents sinful actions; therefore, once healed, the individual must transition from forced restraint to a voluntary choice to remain virtuous.</summary>

### *The Miracle of the Pool of Bethesda: The first time the angels were employed to heal the bodies of men (John 5:2-4).*

That miracle, which consisted only in the healing of the sick, was a prelude to the ordinary miracles of *Christ*. ### *The thirty-eight years of the man with paralysis <note>original: "sick of the Palfey"</note>, followed by another thirty-eight years until the water of the rock dried up.*

Near the start of the wars of the *Romans* against the *Jews*, all the waters around *Jerusalem* completely failed, even those of the Pool of *Siloam*, and consequently those of the Pool of *Bethesda*. This is recounted <note>original: "rehearsed"</note> by *Josephus*, the *Jewish* historian. 

Now, the war of the *Romans* began toward the 69th year of *Christ*. If we count back to his 31st year, in which he healed that man with paralysis, we will find that thirty-eight years passed from that time until the waters of the Pool of *Bethesda* dried up. This is remarkable <note>original: "admirable"</note>: it was the exact same number of years that the man's paralysis had lasted when he was healed right by that pool.

It is true that some years later—namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, around the beginning of the Siege of *Jerusalem*—

It meant that the angel would not come down into it anymore, and indeed, that God had withdrawn himself from *Jerusalem*.

### The number of the wonders Christ performed on the dead, corresponding to the number of the years of his ministry.

### *A remarkable plan <note>original: "admirable dispensation"</note> of God under the New Testament, in which every example of martyrdom is always followed by an example of resurrection. A mysterious exception to that rule.*

In the history of the New Testament, the examples of those who have been killed for the cause of God—including even the little infants from whom he has drawn glory <note>original: "matter of glory"</note>—are listed in chronological order <note>original: "ranked according to the order of the times"</note> as follows:

1. The infants murdered in **Bethlehem**.
2. **John the Baptist** beheaded.
3. **JESUS CHRIST**, the great Martyr and faithful witness, crucified.
4. St. **Stephen** stoned.
5. St. **James**, executed by the sword.
6. **Antipas** put to death for the testimony of **Jesus** (*Revelation* 2:13).

Now, it is remarkable <note>original: "worthy of admiration"</note> that every one of these martyrs was followed by the raising of one or more dead people. After a martyrdom, a resurrection occurred; and again, after a martyrdom, another resurrection followed, and so it continued in alternation <note>original: "alternatively"</note>, even though some time had passed between them,

### *A remarkable arrangement <note>original: "admirable dispensation"; here meaning a wonderful divine plan or ordering of events</note> of God under the New Testament: after every instance of martyrdom, there is always an instance of resurrection. A mysterious exception to that rule.*

In the history of the New Testament, the examples of those who were killed for the cause of God—including even the little infants from whom he has drawn glory—are arranged in chronological order <note>original: "ranked according to the order of the times"</note> as follows:

1. The infants murdered in **Bethlehem**.
2. **John the Baptist** beheaded.
3. **JESUS CHRIST**, the great Martyr and faithful witness, crucified.
4. **St. Stephen** stoned.
5. **St. James**, executed by the sword.
6. **Antipas**, put to death for his testimony for **Jesus** (*Revelation 2:13*).

Now, it is truly remarkable that every one of these martyrs was followed by the raising of one or more dead people. After a martyrdom, a resurrection occurred; and again, after a martyrdom, another resurrection followed. This happened consistently in an alternating pattern, even though there was sometimes an interval of time—

<summary>The author identifies a repeating pattern in the New Testament where every recorded martyrdom is followed by a resurrection, listing six key martyrs starting with the infants of Bethlehem.</summary>

<keywords>martyrdom, resurrection, New Testament, chronology, Bethlehem, John the Baptist, Jesus Christ, St. Stephen, St. James, Antipas</keywords>

--- Page 383 ---
<meta>This page continues the chronological list of New Testament martyrs and the resurrections that followed them, a pattern the author suggests is a divine arrangement.</meta>

[ 75 ]

time, and other occurrences. So it is that since the Massacre of Bethlehem, one martyrdom has not followed another without the intervening raising of some dead person. Observe, then, the order of them in this list <note>original: "Catalogue"</note>.

1. After the martyrdom of the infants of Bethlehem—although it happened a long time afterward, yet within that time there were no other martyrs—there occurred the raising of the young man of Nain and of Jairus’s daughter.

2. After the martyrdom of John the Baptist, the resurrection of Lazarus of Bethany occurred.

3. After the martyrdom of Christ, there happened not only his own resurrection—which was unique to him among all the martyrs—but also the resurrection of many saints who were lying in the dust <note>original: "lying in the dust"; a biblical idiom referring to the dead in their graves</note>.

4. After the martyrdom of St. Stephen, the resurrection of Tabitha occurred (Acts 9).

5. After the martyrdom of St. James, the resurrection of Eutychus <note>original: "Eutych"</note> occurred (Acts 20).

6. But lo, here is a great and remarkable exception; for the last of all the martyrs mentioned in the New Testament—namely <note>original: "viz." (videlicet)</note>, Antipas—is not followed by anyone who has been raised from the dead. That martyr, the last of all, is different from all the previous ones, in that after his death no example of resurrection is found. Is this not to signify,

# The first and the last of those who have been raised from the dead under the New Testament.

Both were young men: the one from *Nain* <note>original: "Naim"; see Luke 7:11-15</note> and *Eutyches* <note>see Acts 20:9-12</note>. Why did that kind of miracle, under the New Testament, begin and end with people of that age? Although those specific resurrections did not grant those individuals perfection or an immortal life, they served as preludes to the final Resurrection, in which our bodies shall receive all possible and appropriate <note>original: "convenient"</note> perfection. Those who died before birth <note>original: "Abortives"</note>, small infants, the dwarf, and the hunched elderly will rise with a stature, vigor, and uprightness corresponding to an age that has passed the imperfections of infancy, but has not yet reached the heaviness <note>original: "corpulency"</note> of a clumsy man nor the defects that usually accompany old age.

It is not useful to argue that there might have been some old men among the saints raised from the dust immediately after *Christ’s* Resurrection; we could provide several answers to that. However, it will be enough to introduce a maxim <note>a short statement expressing a general truth</note> here—which we shall justify elsewhere—that in the Sacred

### *Why the Son of God raised certain dead people without being asked by anyone, while others were raised after being entreated.*

As they were carrying the young man of Nain <note>original: "Naim"; see Luke 7:11–15</note> to be buried, no one—not even his own mother—thought to implore <note>beg or pray for</note> the mercy of Jesus Christ, so that he might be pleased to raise that dead man. Yet, nevertheless, Jesus Christ brought him back to life. But the daughter of Jairus was raised at the entreaty <note>earnest request</note> of her father. It should be observed that before the young man of Nain, Jesus Christ had never raised anyone from the dead. He had made himself known through several cures and other miracles, but not yet by any of that kind. His power had appeared among the living but had not yet extended itself to the dead. Therefore, no petitions <note>original: "addresses"</note> were made to him to request a miracle he had not as <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword "as" suggests the next page continues "as yet performed."</note>

<summary>This page explains that Jesus raised the young man of Nain without being asked because he had not yet demonstrated his power over death; later, as with Jairus's daughter, people began to petition him for such miracles once his power was known.</summary>

### Four degrees of means by which the Son of God manifested his power to the dead.

He raised them either by taking them by the hand, as he did with *Jairus's* daughter; or by touching only the coffin, as with the young man of *Nain* <note>original: "Naim"</note>; or by his word alone, as with *Lazarus*; or by the sole power <note>original: "vertue"</note> of his Spirit, which he transmitted to the graves of those who were lying in the dust of the earth.

### Christ is the first who, in raising the dead, commanded them.

Jewish scholars <note>original: "Doctors"</note> acknowledge that miracles performed through a command were signs of an excellence unique to the person performing them. The meaning of this is that among the procedures or formal methods that God's servants used when performing miracles, the highest method is the one we mention here: specifically, when they commanded the miracle to occur. When a command was followed by the intended result, it showed a special authority given to them from heaven. This placed them in a more eminent position than when they only acted through prayer, by touching, by any other signs, or simply by foretelling the miracle that was to follow.

*Christ* has rebuked fevers <note>original: "Agues"</note>, demons, and the elements: *What manner of man is this, that even the winds and the sea obey him?* Let us say even more: *Who is this man, whom even the dead obey?* When Elijah and his successor *Elisha* were about to perform miracles of that nature, they proceeded by making a request to God and by using lengthy procedures repeated at several intervals; but

<summary>This page argues that Christ’s ability to resurrect the dead by simple command demonstrates a unique divine authority, contrasting his direct power with the prayers and rituals used by prophets like Elijah and Elisha.</summary>
<keywords>miracles, Christ, Jewish scholars, Elijah, Elisha, authority, resurrection, fevers, divine power</keywords>

# A Specific Detail concerning the Daughter of Jairus.

Among all the people raised from the dead, she alone has the number of her years recorded in the History <note>In this context, the author refers to the historical records of the Gospels.</note>; I mean, the number of years she had when she returned from the dead, which is not reported of any other person. Now, I think this may be the reason for that unique detail: the daughter of *Jairus* was the youngest of all the people raised in the New Testament. All the others—the young man of *Naim*, *Eutychus*, *Lazarus*, and especially *Tabitha*—were more advanced in years <note>original: "stricken in years"; meaning elderly or significantly older</note>. We have seen that neither *Christ* nor his Apostles raised any small infants. To the reasons we have already given for this, it may be added that under the New Testament, God was not pleased to perform the miracle of resurrection except upon people who already possessed some discretion <note>original: "discretion"; referring to the "age of discretion" when a person is considered capable of understanding and reasoning about their experiences.</note>, who might have a lasting memory of their return from among the dead. In contrast, an infant cannot judge the greatness of such a wonder, nor keep it in memory; the impression of it is but weak at such a young age and liable to be soon forgotten. But the daughter of *Jairus* had passed beyond her infancy,

infancy, though only a short time before. Because she was the youngest of all those raised from the dead in the New Testament, her specific age is recorded. This informs us that she was already old enough to understand the greatness of the miracle performed on her and to remember it. Therefore, she was capable of testifying to that wonder—which was the intention of *Jesus Christ*.

### The Specific Reason Why John the Baptist Was Removed from the World So Soon.

It was indeed because he had accomplished the work of his calling. But there is a secret here that is clarified <note>original: "rectifi'd"</note> by history. From the time of *John the Baptist's* death, *Christ* remained the only Prophet on earth. It was necessary that the latter part of his ministry <note>original: "Ministery"</note>—of which about a year and a half remained—should be completed without the existence of any other Prophet besides himself. 

God's plan <note>original: "dispensation"</note> is to be considered here; at certain times there have been many prophets in the world, at other times none, and then only one. Such was *Noah* for a while after the Flood; such was *Moses* at the time of the departure from *Egypt*. Being the only Prophet on earth made a man all the more distinguished <note>original: "illustrious"</note>. It was therefore appropriate <note>original: "convenient"</note> that the Prince of the Prophets should have a time when there

### Why the Son of God, going to his disciples who were on the sea, did not make a path for himself through the waters, but walked upon them.

The waters had given way to the *Israelites* in the *Red Sea*, and later when the *Jordan* River divided itself to let them enter *Canaan* under the leadership <note>original: "conduct"</note> of *Joshua*. It happened again for *Elijah* when he crossed that river to ascend into Heaven, and once more for *Elisha* when he returned from *Elijah*. Thus, that specific kind of miracle—the dividing of the waters—had occurred four times. 

But the Son of God chose <note>original: "was pleased"</note> to perform a miracle on that element that was unknown and unheard of until then: to be carried over the waters and supported by them, either by making them solid or by some other means that enabled them to bear a man's body, or perhaps being upheld solely by his own power. It was a completely new miracle, which he chose to perform rather than repeat a miracle that had already occurred several times, namely <note>original: "viz." (abbreviation for the Latin *videlicet*)</note> the division of the waters. This provides us with the opportunity to include the following observation.

<summary>This page discusses why Jesus chose to walk on water rather than part it, noting that while the parting of waters had occurred four times in the Old Testament, walking on water was a unique and unprecedented miracle.</summary>

# Of all the types of miracles that occurred under the Old Testament, Christ, while still on Earth, only repeated seven of them and gave them a broader reach <note>original: "extent"</note> than they had previously.

The seven types of miracles which occurred under the Old Testament were: 

1. The shining face of *Moses*. 
2. The cloud that covered the Tabernacle and which filled the Temple at its dedication under *Solomon*. 
3. *Miriam* and *Naaman* being healed of their leprosy. 
4. The outpouring <note>original: "effusion"</note> of the Holy Spirit on the seventy men (*Numbers* 11:25). 
5. The healing of *Jeroboam's* withered hand. 
6. The dead being raised by *Elijah* and *Elisha*. 
7. Food being multiplied by those two prophets.

We do not include <note>original: "reckon"</note> here the miraculous forty-day fast of *Moses*, *Elijah*, and *Christ*. For the miracles of our Lord—performed after he entered his ministry following his time in the wilderness—began at *Cana*, where he performed <note>original: "wrought"</note> his first miracle. Let us, therefore, examine the seven types of miracles:

**Christ** healed the withered hand of a man who had been deprived of its use <note>original: "the use thereof"</note> for a longer time than **Jeroboam**.

<column-break/>

6 Three dead people were raised under the Old Testament; all three had been dead for only a short time.

<column-break/>

**Christ** raised not only three dead people—one of whom had been in the grave for four days <note>Lazarus</note>—but also many bodies of the saints who were lying in the dust.

<column-break/>

7 **Elijah** multiplied the widow’s oil and flour <note>original: "Meal"</note> to feed her and her son (*1 Kings 17*). **Elisha** multiplied the oil in the house of a poor woman. That same prophet, having only twenty loaves, fed a great number of men with them.

<column-break/>

**Christ**, having only five loaves and two small fishes, fed five thousand men, along with a great number of women and children. Another time, having only seven loaves and a few small fishes, he fed four thousand men who had been without food <note>original: "without eating"</note> for three days, as well as a great number of women and children.

---

In most of these parallels, it can be seen regarding these seven kinds of miracles that occurred in the Old Testament, that **Christ** repeated <note>original: "reiterated"</note> them with even greater power and significance <note>original: "with advantage"</note> in many respects.

# Again, the 32nd year of Christ.

### *The third feast of the Passover that occurred since his baptism, and some events that followed it.*

## CONSIDERATIONS.
### *Why Christ, who raised several people from the dead, healed only one deaf man? And why he used more ceremonies <note>original: "formalities"</note> and spent more time in giving hearing to that deaf man than in giving life to the dead.*

If the greatest miracles had always been the least frequent, some might have imagined that it had been harder for God to perform them than to produce lesser wonders—as if his power were limited by the scale <note>original: "proportion"</note> he gives to his works. But to show that the greatest wonders are equally easy for him, he gave more examples of them in some types of miracles than in others that were much smaller. To raise a dead man is a greater

...greater miracle than to give hearing to many deaf men; and yet the Son of God, who raised many from the dead during the time of his ministry, gave hearing to only one deaf person (*Mark 7:32, etc.*). Let us add this as well: to raise a dead man is a greater miracle than to cure a sick man, even one who is dying. Yet out of all the sick people the Son of God healed, we find only one who was near death—namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, the Centurion’s servant—but many who were truly dead were raised by Christ.

That deaf man, whom the chronological order of events <note>original: "order of the times"</note> leads us to describe here, also had an impediment in his speech. Although the wonder of his recovery of hearing and speech was great, it was nevertheless a lesser miracle than the raising of a dead man. And yet, for the healing of that man, the Son of God used more ceremonies <note>original: "formalities"</note>, which were much longer and more varied, than he ever did for any of the dead he raised. He takes this patient, draws him aside, puts his finger into his ears, touches his tongue, and applies his saliva <note>original: "spittle"</note> to him. By this he was pleased to show that the signs or ceremonies he used were chosen freely <note>original: "arbitrary"</note>, and that his power was

### Some types of miracles Christ reserved for himself during the time of his ministry.

While he was on the earth, no one else ever gave sight to the blind, and no one else raised the dead. He gave power to his disciples—first to the twelve, and then to the seventy—to cast out demons <note>original: "Devils"</note> and to heal all sorts of diseases and illnesses <note>original: "infirmities"</note>. However, their authority <note>original: "commission"</note> did not extend to giving sight to the blind, much less to giving life to the dead. At least, we do not read that his disciples, while still in this world, had ever performed a miracle of either of those two kinds. Christ reserved some—chiefly those two, which are the greatest—so that there might be some signs of his special authority <note>original: "Prerogative"</note> to create a distinction between the Master of Miracles and those

### *Whatsoever you shall bind on Earth, shall be bound in Heaven, etc.*

Allow me to offer a new interpretation <note>original: "Exposition"</note>, or at least a conjecture, regarding these words: This "binding" and "loosing" does not refer to people (as if they were to be physically bound or loosed), nor to things (as if "binding" meant to forbid and "loosing" meant to permit, which is the interpretation of a learned modern author). However, the words immediately preceding these in the text—*I will give unto you the keys of the kingdom of heaven*—shed some light on the matter. It has been observed that during the ceremonies <note>original: "Solemnities"</note> used by the *Jews* when appointing a Doctor of the Law, certain keys were placed in his hands. These were the keys to the archives, or the places where the copies of

<summary>The author argues that certain miracles were reserved solely for Christ and proposes that "binding and loosing" refers to the Jewish tradition of giving a Doctor of the Law keys to sacred archives rather than a general power to permit or forbid.</summary>

<keywords>miracles, Christ's authority, Ascension, binding and loosing, keys of the kingdom, Jewish law, Doctor of the Law, biblical interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 406 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "keys of the kingdom," specifically how the ceremony for appointing a Jewish Doctor of the Law relates to St. Peter's authority.</meta>

of the Sacred Books were kept; and it was as much as to say that the new Doctor <note>original: "Doctor"; here meaning a learned teacher or scholar of religious law</note> had power henceforth publicly to read and teach. *Jesus Christ* makes an allusion to that ceremony, promising *St. Peter* to make him a Doctor of the Gospel. This has been observed by our predecessors, but we may add to it what follows.

It is known what the form of books of old was: they were made in the manner of rolls <note>scrolls</note>, and necessarily tied with some small string to avoid the tangling <note>original: "imbroiling"</note> of the margins and to keep them in good condition. When anyone was willing to read from them, they used to untie them; and when a long break from reading was intended, they would tie them again. Now the keys promised by *Christ* to *St. Peter* were the keys that open the door to the Doctors of the Gospel, so that they might take the Book out and might tie and untie it. That is to say, they might keep it shut or open it as it shall be convenient. The first (tying) is done when they refuse unbelievers and unrepentant persons the application <note>the offer or benefit</note> of the promises contained in the Gospel. The second (untying) is done for those of a contrary disposition. We see an example of the first in *St. Peter* himself, when he said to *Simon the Magician*, "*You have neither part nor lot in this matter*,"

<summary>The author explains that "binding and loosing" refers to the ancient practice of tying and untying scroll-books, symbolizing a teacher's authority to either offer or withhold the Gospel's promises based on a person's faith.</summary>

...matter, namely <note>original: "viz.", short for the Latin *videlicet*</note> in the teachings of the Gospel. Then that Book was tied shut to that hypocrite <note>referring to Simon the Magician from the previous page</note>. That which is tied and untied is indeed one and the same Book, but it is distinguished from itself by the difference between those two actions. That which is a scent <note>original: "favour", referring to the "savor" or aroma of the Gospel mentioned in 2 Corinthians 2:16</note> of life to some, and a scent of death to others, is nonetheless one and the same Gospel.

### Concerning Christ’s *Transfiguration*; and first, the four groups <note>original: "Orders"</note> of men found there.

In this glorious event, there were:
1. Living men who were destined to die. 
2. One who had been dead and was returned to life again. 
3. One who never died, and yet ascended bodily into Heaven. 
4. One who was to die, return to life, and bodily ascend into Heaven.

The living people who were destined to die were the three Apostles who accompanied our Lord.

The one who had been dead and was returned to life again was *Moses*.

The one who had never died, and yet had bodily ascended into Heaven, was *Elijah*.
