<meta>This page continues the analysis of the figures present at the Transfiguration, identifying Jesus as the fourth person and explaining how this assembly represents the entire Church in its various states.</meta>

He who was to die, return to life, and bodily ascend into Heaven was *Jesus Christ* himself.

That assembly was the summary <note>original: "Epitome"</note> of the universal Church, considered in different <note>original: "divers"</note> conditions and at various times.

1. The three Apostles, who were still subject to persecutions and to death, represented the Church as it is still militant <note>The "Church Militant" refers to Christians on earth who are currently struggling against sin, the world, and the devil</note>.

2. He who had been dead and was returned to life <note>referring to Moses</note> represented the believers who shall rise at the last day.

3. He who had ascended into Heaven without experiencing <note>original: "tasting"</note> death <note>referring to Elijah</note> represented those who shall be found alive at the last day and shall not die, but shall be transformed <note>original: "changed"</note>.

4. All these three groups <note>original: "Orders"</note> belong to <note>original: "hold of"</note> *Jesus Christ*, who is the head of the Church, of both the living and the dead; of those who shall rise and of those who shall be transformed. In the persons of those who appeared at his Transfiguration, the whole Church appeared before him. Without Christ, that assembly could not be complete, nor even formed.

# A Distinction between the three Apostles who were spectators of the Transfiguration of our Lord.

### *A Significant Mystery in the Appearance of* Elijah *and* Moses.

Several reasons are given for God’s choice of those two prophets to appear in that assembly, where those three persons <note>Jesus, Moses, and Elijah all fasted for forty days: Moses on Sinai, Elijah on the way to Horeb, and Jesus in the wilderness.</note> who were famous for a fast of forty days met together. But let us add the following observations to those reasons.

The bodies of those two great prophets had formerly been miraculously taken out of the sight of men, and miraculously they then appeared again. The body of *Elijah* was taken up into Heaven; men looked for it for a while, but in vain <note>2 Kings 2:15–18 describes how fifty men searched for Elijah's body after he was taken up in a whirlwind but could not find it.</note>. *Moses’* <note>original: "Moses his"</note> body was never seen dead, for God took it out of the sight of men and, with his own hands, placed him in a grave that remains unknown to this very day <note>Deuteronomy 34:6</note>. Thus, since the bodies of those two prophets were brought back into view, it must have been for a very extraordinary reason, as we shall see in the following observation.

### Three Kinds of Witnesses: 1. From Heaven, 2. From Earth, 3. From Under the Earth.

In the Transfiguration of our Lord, there appeared:
1. A man come from heaven, namely Elijah.
2. Three men still residing on the earth, namely the three Apostles.
3. A man come from under the earth, namely Moses.

Thus, the glory in which our Lord then appeared has had witnesses come from all parts of the world: from heaven, from earth, and from under the earth (that is, from the grave). This was a preview <note>original: "prelude"</note> of Christ's Exaltation, before whom "every knee shall bow, of those in heaven, and of those on earth, and of those under the earth" <note>A reference to the Apostle Paul’s words in Philippians 2:10 regarding the supreme authority of Jesus.</note>.

### Why were they chosen to be present at the Transfiguration of our Lord?

It was necessary to have one witness from Heaven and one from the grave, as we just mentioned. However, in heaven, there were only two men present in both body and soul: namely, Enoch <note>original: "Enos"; the author likely refers to Enoch, who was "translated" to heaven without dying (Genesis 5:24).</note> and *Elijah*. All others who were there existed only as spirits or souls. Of those two who were physically in heaven, God sent down the one who had lived closest to the time of *Christ*, and the one whom the forerunner of *Christ*, *John the Baptist*, most closely resembled.

Furthermore, there was a common belief <note>original: "prejudice"</note> among the *Jews* that *Elijah* was destined to return in person before the *Messiah* appeared. Therefore, *Elijah*—the very prophet they thought would personally precede *Christ*—came at that moment to testify that *Christ* had already arrived. It was fitting that the common error concerning *Elijah* should be corrected by *Elijah* himself before *Christ* departed from the world. Indeed, the time of His departure was drawing near when *Elijah* appeared at His Transfiguration.

As for those who were in the grave, *Moses* had this unique distinction: no one else was ever buried by God’s own

<summary>This page explains that Elijah represented heaven because he was one of the few physically present there, and his appearance corrected Jewish misconceptions about his return. Moses is introduced as the representative of the dead due to his unique burial by God.</summary>
<keywords>Transfiguration, Elijah, Enoch, John the Baptist, Jewish prophecy, Messiah, Moses, bodily assumption</keywords>

--- Page 413 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding Moses' burial and the reasons why he and Elijah were chosen to appear at the Transfiguration.</meta>

own hands. From this it followed that he could not be moved or taken out of his grave except by the same hand that had placed him there and had even hidden the location. Since he was brought back to sight, it was necessary for some great event for which God had reserved him. But since it was to give testimony to *Christ*, *Christ’s* dignity appeared all the more clearly because of it.

### Elijah did not return to the world until after John the Baptist had left it; and why?

When *Elijah* appeared at the Transfiguration, *John the Baptist* had already died. The wisdom of God—though we cannot know all His reasons—would not permit there to be two *Elijahs* in the world at the same time; for *John the Baptist* was also an *Elijah* <note>original: "an Elijah"; John the Baptist was considered the spiritual successor to Elijah, fulfilling the prophecy in Malachi 4:5 that Elijah would return before the "great and terrible day of the Lord."</note>. This was not because they were incompatible, since <note>original: "sith"</note> the one came in the spirit and power <note>original: "vertue"</note> of the other. Instead, it is likely because it should not seem as though the testimony given by *John the Baptist* had been suggested to him by *Elijah*—as if by a master to his disciple—rather than coming directly <note>original: "immediately"; in this context, it means without an intermediary or coming straight from God.</note>

<summary>This page explains that Moses was uniquely reserved by God for the Transfiguration and argues that Elijah only appeared after John the Baptist's death to ensure the Baptist's testimony about Christ was seen as divine rather than learned from the original prophet.</summary>

And it was very important that his testimony should be attributed to the direct calling of God, just as the calling of the Apostles later was (see *Galatians* 1:1).

### The Assembly of those who appeared at the Transfiguration of our Lord, and their distinction.

That assembly consisted of seven persons: specifically, three disciples, two prophets, the head of the prophets, and He who presided among them, namely, God the Father.

### *The voice from the midst of the Cloud.*

Formerly, when God was speaking to *Moses*, the voice came from the bottom of a cloud (*Exodus 33; Numbers 11 and 12*). But now, in the presence and in the hearing of *Moses*, God spoke out of the bottom of a cloud. The disciples who were present at the Transfiguration of *Christ*, and to whom the voice was directed, were instructed by as glorious a means as that by which *Moses* formerly received his instructions (*Hebrews 2:3*).

### *...ous in miracles than the previous ones.*

**T**his is evident. Previously, he raised bodies that were still intact and had not yet seen decay <note>original: "corruption"</note>; but hereafter, he will raise those that were already decomposing <note>original: "putrifi'd"</note>. The closer he came to the end of his ministry, the more he intensified his miracles, bringing them to the highest possible level. And we shall see those miracles he performed at the very time of his death.

### Luke 9:56

The reason for that refusal was that the Son of Man had not come to destroy men's lives; for, from that principle, he concludes that he should not allow his disciples to bring about the death of the *Samaritans*. But when Saint *Peter* caused *Ananias* and *Sapphira* to die—which happened later—was it not by the permission, and indeed by the commission <note>commission: a formal granting of authority or a specific task to perform</note>, of the Son of God, and by virtue of the power to work miracles that he gave to that Apostle? Did the same reason for which he would not cause the *Samaritans* to die not apply likewise to *Ananias* and *Sapphira*?

Here we must distinguish between the different times. While *Christ* was on earth, living among mortal men, he would perform no acts other than those of mercy toward them, causing those who were dying to live, and those who were already dead to live again. But after his Ascension into heaven, it was necessary that he should also perform some acts of justice to show that he is at the right hand of God, and that one day he will judge the secrets of men—just as he then judged the conspiracy <note>original: "plot"</note> of *Ananias* and *Sapphira*. On earth, he practiced only mercy,

### *The first Temple was violated by the Jews, who stoned a Prophet within it; and the second Temple was violated by the Jews when they attempted to stone to death the Head of the Prophets.*

Both Temples became detestable <note>original: "abominable"</note> for many reasons, but particularly for these two crimes of the same kind. The *Jews* maintain that the primary cause of the destruction of the first Temple was the death of *Zechariah*, the son of *Jehoiada*, whom their ancestors <note>original: "fathers"</note> stoned to death in its courtyard. Yet that Temple stood for 1,260 years <note>The author's chronology here reflects theological calculations common in the 17th century rather than modern historical dating.</note> after the death of *Zechariah*. What reason will they give for the destruction of the second Temple, which lasted only 40 years after the *Jews* attempted to stone *Jesus Christ* within it? (See John 8:59). Indeed, the ruin of that second Temple was more absolute than that of the first, as we shall see shortly.

<summary>The author compares the destruction of the two Jewish Temples, arguing that both were ruined because of the mistreatment of prophets—specifically Zechariah in the first and Jesus in the second.</summary>
<keywords>First Temple, Second Temple, Zechariah, Jehoiada, Jesus Christ, John 8:59, divine judgment, Jewish history</keywords>

--- Page 424 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the destruction of the Second Temple as divine retribution for the attempted stoning of Jesus Christ.</meta>

### *In the Temple, where the Jews took up stones to stone Jesus Christ, not one stone stood upon another.*

That Temple was not only burned, as the first one was, but even the place where it stood was brought to such a state that there remained no mark of any building—not even of the very stones themselves. History reports that the *Romans* turned the site into a tillable or cultivated <note>original: "manured"</note> field; indeed, they even caused the plow to pass over it. And so, not only did the Temple vanish away, but even its very pavement, the stones of which had been used for such a horrific attempt <note>Referring to the attempt to stone Jesus mentioned in the heading.</note>.

These two miracles are: 1. The healing of the woman who had been bent double <note>original: "bowed together"</note> for many years (Luke 13). 2. The giving of sight to a man who was born blind (John 9). Both of these were noted ahead of time, over a thousand years earlier, and joined together in the very same Psalm and in the very same verse: Psalm 146:8. *The Lord opens the eyes of the blind; the Lord raises those who are bowed down.* Furthermore, both of these occurred on a Sabbath day, as we shall see.

The other miracle, the healing of the woman who was bent over <note>original: "crooked"</note>, occurred before this one and followed shortly after the discourse *Jesus Christ* had given concerning the eighteen men whom the Tower in *Siloam* killed <note>original: "slew"</note>. Do the eighteen years of that woman's infirmity (*Luke* 13:11) not suggest a connection <note>original: "analogy"</note>? It seems to serve as a memorial of that wonderful cure, ending a chronic <note>original: "inveterate"</note> disease that had lasted as many years as there were men slain—whom *Jesus Christ* had only recently mentioned.

These circumstances did not happen by chance, and the details the Evangelists <note>the authors of the Gospels: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John</note> have recorded cannot be considered meaningless <note>original: "idle"</note>. The places, times, and people involved have connections that ought to be carefully considered. It is useless for anyone to ask how a disease of eighteen years could so precisely coincide with a discussion about the death of eighteen men. For Divine Providence so extends and prepares events in advance that they occur at the exact moment he has appointed <note>original: "prefixed"</note>.

## The time of year Christ gave sight to that blind man.

seasons; but miraculous acts are not subject to the time of year. Indeed, what spring or harvest-time could have contributed to the production of such a wonder as this?

It was in winter when the Son of God healed the man with dropsy <note>original: "sick of the Dropsy"; a historical term for edema, or swelling caused by fluid retention</note> mentioned in the fourteenth chapter of *Luke*. It was near the end of winter when he performed the first cure of a man with palsy <note>original: "sick of the Palsey"; an archaic term for paralysis or tremors</note>. Anyone who observes the timelines recorded in the New Testament and noted by commentators <note>original: "Interpreters"</note> will find that these healings, and others like them, occurred during the winter season.

And regarding that blind man: what help could the treatment prescribed to him—to wash in water—have provided when the harshness <note>original: "rigour"</note> of the season might instead have made it harmful and dangerous to him in other ways?

That blind man was poor—even a beggar (*John 9:8*). Another blind man...

man whom our Lord cured was also reduced to beggary (*Mark 10:46*). But *Jesus Christ* did give alms, and one of the Apostles was the dispenser of them (*John 13:29*). Yet, he chose not to have alms given to those poor blind men. Would it not have been a double act of kindness <note>original: "beneficence"</note> if, when he gave them sight, he had also provided them with the means to satisfy their hunger? But since even the best actions are most often subject to criticism <note>original: "detraction"</note>, *Jesus Christ* would not have lacked adversaries who would have disputed the truth of the miracle. They might have said that perhaps those beggars were never blind at all, but only pretended <note>original: "feigned"</note> to have been so because they were hired with money.

This leads to the following observation.

### A Digression on the Miscalculations of Those who Confuse the Seasons of the Year which the Evangelists Distinguished.

IN several orthodox <note>In this context, "orthodox" likely refers to established or traditional Protestant churches rather than the Eastern Orthodox Church specifically.</note> churches, the custom is still maintained of publicly reading specific passages of Scripture once a year, with each passage assigned to a particular day. <meta>This page continues the discussion on liturgical readings, specifically criticizing the tradition of reading the "Good Shepherd" passage in the spring. It argues that while the imagery fits spring, the biblical text (John 10:22) explicitly places the event in winter.</meta>

Therefore, the reading of that passage is done in the spring—and very appropriately <note>original: "seasonably"</note>, as a learned theologian <note>original: "Divine"</note> of ours has written. He says this is because spring is the season when the shepherds and their flocks begin to go out into the fields.

However, it should be noted that those words of *Jesus Christ* were spoken in the winter, more than three months before the arrival of spring (John 10:22). Therefore, that metaphor <note>original: "Simile"</note> was not chosen because of the time of year. We know that any part of Scripture may be read at any time whatsoever; yet it should be done with wisdom, especially during public worship <note>original: "Exercises"</note>. It is particularly important for us to observe the specific times when *Jesus Christ* spoke or acted; otherwise, we can have only a very unclear and confused knowledge of the history of the Gospel, which leads to several problems <note>original: "inconveniences"</note>.

the merchants, once around the time of the first Passover of his ministry, and the second time, around the fourth and final Passover. The first time, he only rebuked them for making the Temple a marketplace (John 2); but the other time, he charged them with a crime of a different and more serious nature—namely, having made the Temple a "den of thieves." In my opinion, the reason for this may be as follows:

The first time our Lord expelled these merchants, no one had yet attempted to stone him in the Temple; therefore, he only called it a marketplace at that time. But since they had afterward attempted to spill the blood of the righteous within its walls, that place deserved to be called a "den of thieves" (Matthew 21:13). It seems that although our Lord specifically pointed to the buying and selling practiced in the Temple, calling that "theft" and "robbery" would be a great exaggeration <note>original: "Hyperbole"</note> if the term did not imply a more serious crime than simple trading. I am referring to a crime called by its proper name and already committed by the **Jews**: for they had truly acted like robbers in the Temple when they attempted to murder **Jesus Christ** there on two occasions. And it often happened that when God punished <note>original: "visiting"</note> lesser sins <note>original: "iniquities"</note>, he did not express the same level of severity in his rebuke <note>original: "censure,"</note>

## The Parabolic History of the Rich Man <note>original: "Dives," which is the Latin word for "rich man" and was often used as a proper name in early English translations of this parable</note> and **Lazarus**.

### The first observation.

When the Scripture speaks of one who is in hell: if hell is named, the person is not named; but if the person is named, hell is not named at all.

So the name of hell being expressly mentioned in that

<summary>The author concludes that Jesus' harsh "den of thieves" rebuke was a response to attempted murder in the Temple, before moving on to discuss Christ's final Sabbath miracle and the naming conventions used in the parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus.</summary>

--- Page 437 ---
<meta>This page continues the first observation regarding the parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus, specifically discussing how the Bible avoids naming individuals currently in hell to caution against human judgment. It then begins a second observation about the social conditions of the afterlife.</meta>

in that parable, the proper name of that rich man is not expressed. On the contrary, when the "son of perdition," *Judas*, is named after his death, he is not said to be in hell; it only says that *he was gone to his own place* (Acts 1:25). Similarly, we know that there are some spirits in prison (1 Peter 3:19), but not one of them is named in that Scripture when it speaks of their prison. This silence of the Holy Spirit <note>original: "holy Ghost"</note> condemns the rashness of those who prematurely assign *Hell* as the habitation of a soul, whom God alone has the power to send into hellfire.

->*The second Observation.*<-

Three souls are represented here: only one in the place of torment, and two others—namely <note>original: "viz." (videlicet)</note>, *Abraham* and *Lazarus*—in the place of Rest. Yet the soul of that rich man <note>original: "Dives," the Latin word for "rich man"</note> is not alone in hell. However, those who are in that place of torment have no affection for one another, nor is any of them comforted by the company <note>original: "society"</note> of those who are with him. On the contrary, those who are in Bliss rejoice to be there in company, even in that of the Patriarchs, and they create there a wonderful harmony <note>original: "admirable consort," referring to a harmonious musical ensemble or deep companionship</note>.

<summary>The author observes that the Bible avoids naming specific individuals in hell as a warning against human judgment, and contrasts the isolation and lack of affection in eternal torment with the joyful, harmonious community found in Bliss.</summary>
<keywords>Rich Man and Lazarus, Judas, Acts 1:25, 1 Peter 3:19, hell, afterlife, Holy Spirit, Abraham, Patriarchs, Dives</keywords>

->*The Third Observation.*<-

The soul of that rich man <note>original: "Dives," the Latin word for "rich man" often used as a proper name in this context</note>, being in hell, makes no appeals <note>original: "addresses"</note> to God, but only to *Abraham*. Indeed, he does not even pronounce the name of God; either because those who are in hell have utterly forgotten God, or because they expect no comfort from him, or else because they hate <note>original: "abhor"</note> him and speak of him only through blasphemy. The parable is full of wisdom.

->*The Fourth Observation.*<-

When *Moses* came into the world, *Abraham* had already left it 250 years earlier; therefore, this Patriarch had never seen *Moses’s* <note>original: "Moses his"</note> writings, nor the writings of any of the Prophets who followed *Moses*. But *Abraham’s* voice recommending the Scriptures is the voice of God himself, signifying that we are not to expect any other guidance <note>original: "addresses"</note>—either from the early Church Fathers <note>original: "the Fathers"</note> or from any others—except for those provided to us by the Scriptures. And truly, *Abraham* is represented here speaking like *Isaiah*, who, while forbidding people to consult the dead, sends us to the *Law and the Testimony* (Isaiah 8:19, 20).

### *The fifth Observation.*

*Abraham* says that although *Lazarus*, or any other of the dead, should rise, the living would not be the better for it. Has this not been verified in another *Lazarus*—the one from *Bethany*—whom our Lord raised from the dead soon after? Not only was it debated whether to send him back to the grave <note>According to John 12:10, the chief priests plotted to kill the resurrected Lazarus because his return to life caused many people to believe in Jesus</note>, but they also brought about the death of the one who had restored him to life.

Undoubtedly, when *Christ* spoke that parable, he was referring to that second *Lazarus* whom he intended to raise from the dead soon after, for that miracle happened only a few days later. The effect of it, as seen in many of the leaders among the *Jews*, was nothing other than their hearts being hardened—a reaction expressed here by *Abraham*. In that regard, the parable has become a history; this is even more clearly seen because a man named *Lazarus* actually has been raised from the dead.

<summary>The author argues that Jesus's parable predicted the real-life reaction to the resurrection of Lazarus of Bethany, demonstrating that even a miracle cannot convince those who are determined to remain hardened in their hearts.</summary>
<keywords>Abraham, Lazarus of Bethany, Parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus, resurrection, hardening of hearts, Jewish leaders, miracles</keywords>

### *The Petition of the two blind men not far from Jericho, Matthew 20:30, etc.*

One of the greatest desires of the Patriarchs, Kings, and Prophets was to see the *Christ*. *Simeon*, once he had seen him, was satisfied never to see anything else again <note>In Luke 2, Simeon declares he can depart in peace because he has seen the Savior.</note>. These blind men, who made their appeal to the Son of God, should have only desired their sight so that they might see him—even if they never saw another object again, and even if they lost their eyes immediately after seeing him once. However, most people who have sought his assistance have been lacking or otherwise misguided <note>original: "wanting, or otherwise irregular"</note> in their requests.

### Why Jesus Christ Wept for Lazarus, but Did Not Weep for John the Baptist?

Several causes are alleged <note>original: "alledged"</note> for the tears *Christ* shed at *Lazarus's* <note>original: "Lazarus his"</note> grave. ### The seven days immediately preceding *Christ’s* Resurrection.

**I**t is <note>original: "TIs"</note> observable that, according to the Scripture, the custom of the *Jews*, and the style of the Evangelists, the day began at sunset <note>original: "Sun-setting"</note>. The following hours—of which the first are those of the night—belong to the same day that began at the setting of the sun and extends itself to the following evening. According to that account, which is known to be the most ancient <note>original: "antientest"</note>, the Passover which our Lord celebrated, his agony in the Garden, his death, and the circumstances surrounding it, all happened within one and the same day. Similarly, his entry into *Jerusalem* did not happen the day after the supper made for him in *Bethany*, even though there was a night between the two events; rather, it occurred within the same day. For want of observing that rule, many calculate inaccurately <note>original: "take their measures ill"</note> regarding the timeline and the distinctions that history provides on this subject.

<summary>The author explains the Jewish custom of beginning the day at sunset, noting that this chronological rule is essential for correctly understanding the timing of the events of the Passion, such as the Passover and the Crucifixion.</summary>

# The Seven Days Immediately Preceding Christ’s Resurrection.

It is worth noting that according to Scripture, the custom of the *Jews*, and the style used by the Evangelists <note>The authors of the four Gospels: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John.</note>, the day began at sunset. The hours that followed, the first of which are those of the night, belong to that same day—which began at the setting of the sun and extended until the following evening. According to that reckoning <note>original: "account"</note>, which is known to be the most ancient, the Passover that our Lord celebrated, his agony in the Garden, his death, and the circumstances surrounding it, all happened within one and the same day. 

Thus, his entry into *Jerusalem* did not happen the day after the supper held for him in *Bethany*, even though a night passed in between; rather, it occurred within the same day. Because many people fail to observe this rule, they often miscalculate the timeline <note>original: "take their measures ill"</note> and the distinctions that the history provides on this subject.

<summary>The author explains that the biblical and Jewish day begins at sunset, meaning that events like the Last Supper, Christ's agony, and his death all occurred within a single twenty-four-hour period.</summary>
<keywords>Chronology, Jewish custom, Evangelists, Passover, Jerusalem, Bethany, Passion of Christ, Timekeeping, Resurrection</keywords>

## The First Day

### *A dead man was raised, and he who raised him ate together at one table.*

This happened in *Bethany* (*John* 12:1, etc.). There *Jesus Christ* and *Lazarus* met—the man whom he had raised from the dead just a few days before. If the people involved are considered <note>original: "taken notice of"</note>, it is one of the most remarkable banquets that ever occurred; there, one could see a man who had recently come from among the dead eating with the living. The ordinary way for men is to go from the table to the grave, but this man went from the grave to the table. There was also to be seen the Author of that miracle <note>original: "wonder"</note>, making these two very admirable figures to behold.

---

### *Mary's intention in anointing the feet of Jesus Christ.*

It may be that her intention <note>original: "it"</note> went no further than to express her gratitude to him for giving *Lazarus* back to her; but Divine Providence <note>The belief that God intervenes in the world to direct events toward a specific outcome.</note> brought another interpretation out of it.

interpretation, although **Mary** did not think then of **Christ's** burial <note>original: "funerals"</note>, and she likely wished him a long life on earth. Her devotion <note>original: "piety"</note> was the reason that this intention is attributed to her. Similarly, the blessing given by **Isaac** to **Jacob**—even though he intended to give it to **Esau**—is also credited to the faith of that Patriarch, as if his intention had actually been to bless **Jacob** (Hebrews 11:20).

### *If these should keep silent, the stones would cry out*, Luke 19:40.

This hyperbole <note>original: "Hyperbole"; an exaggerated statement or figure of speech not meant to be taken literally.</note> became historical fact six days later, for it was fulfilled at **Christ's** death. When no one spoke up for him, the rocks split, and it is very likely they did so with a great noise. Nevertheless, the stones "opened their mouths" to condemn those people whose mouths were shut at a time when they should have been opened to give glory to the **Son of God**.

### *The short interval of time between Christ’s driving out those selling in the Temple and the tearing of the Temple’s veil.*

On the first of those seven days preceding his Resurrection, he disciplined those who profaned the Temple, and on the sixth day—namely, that of his death—the tearing of the veil <note>original: "Rent of the Vail"</note> occurred to signify that the service of the Temple was abolished. But why was our Lord so angry at the profanation of a service which was only to remain legitimate for six days longer?

The question provides its own answer. The closer the ceremonies of the Temple came to their end, the more religiously they were to be observed. Because the death of *Christ* was represented by the sacrifices, it was necessary that those types <term>types: symbolic rituals or persons in the Old Testament that foreshadowed Christ</term> be handled and presented with all reverence and holiness. Above all, when they came nearer to that perfect sacrifice—the death of *Christ*—then they were to be displayed even more holily in the presence of their prototype <note>original: "Prototype"; the original reality that the "types" or symbols were designed to represent.</note>.

<summary>This page explains why Jesus maintained the sanctity of the Temple rituals just days before they were to be abolished, arguing that symbols of Christ's sacrifice required the greatest reverence as they reached their fulfillment.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus Christ, Temple cleansing, Tearing of the Veil, Resurrection, Passion Week, Typology, Sacrifice, Prototype</keywords>

# The Blind and the Cripple came to the Temple to Christ.

Those two types of disabled <note>original: "impotent"</note> people never entered *David’s* Palace, but were always barred from entering it (2 *Samuel* 5:8). Yet God permitted them to enter his Temple. Nonetheless, none of them were ever healed there until the Son of God performed that miracle; and that occurred only once, a few days before his death. In the Temple, he healed only the blind and the lame <note>original: "creeple"</note>; for indeed, no others presented themselves to him there.

---

# The third time the Son of God was proclaimed from Heaven by the voice of his Father.

The first time was at his baptism, when God declared that he was well-pleased with his Son.

The second was at his Transfiguration, when God added that they should listen to his Son.

The progression <note>original: "gradation"</note> in these three proclamations is clear enough. The variety of times and occasions when they occurred shows the reasons for the differences in their content.

## The Second Day
### *Regarding the Cursing of the Fig Tree; Defending the Common Reading of that Text Against Certain Modern Scholars*

In the Gospel of Mark, chapter 11, verse 13, the Evangelist says that *Jesus Christ* found only leaves on that fig tree, then adds these words: "*For the time for figs had not yet come.*" However, some scholars read it differently: "*For the time for figs was then.*" Their reason for departing from the standard reading and substituting words that are the exact opposite is, they claim, because at that season of the year—namely <note>original: "viz."</note> around the Feast of the Passover—figs were already ripe in *Palestine*, given the heat of that climate.

<summary>This page explores the divine proclamations of Christ's identity and begins a scholarly defense of the traditional translation of Mark 11:13 regarding the cursing of the fig tree.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus Christ, Jerusalem, John 12:28, Mark 11:13, Fig Tree, Passover, Palestine, Biblical Criticism, Translation</keywords>

--- Page 453 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the timing of the fig harvest in Judea, specifically addressing why Jesus might have expected fruit out of the main season.</meta>

climate, and that the harvest also began then.

But these men have either forgotten, or were ignorant of, the various <note>original: "diverse"</note> kinds of that fruit, which grow at very different seasons of the year. For in countries much further north <note>original: "less Southerly"</note> than *Judea*, there are some fig trees where the fruit is ripe before the summer solstice, and others that bear ripe fruit only four months later. Thus, some already provide nourishment to people when others have not yet even put forth their buds. Who doubts that the same may be true in *Judea*? 

Therefore, the objection that there were some ripe figs in the spring can only be applied to certain kinds of that fruit, and not to the later variety, of which it is said that the time for figs had not yet come. Consequently, *Jesus Christ* encountered one of those fig trees whose time to bear fruit had not yet arrived. This is how the phrase should be understood when it is said that "the time for figs had not yet come"—namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, it refers to those figs of the specific kind of fig tree where *Jesus Christ* looked for fruit. In general, figs have more than one season.

It is noteworthy that those who support an affirmative reading of this text—claiming it says, "It *was* the time for figs"—make the Evangelist <note>The author of the Gospel, in this case, Mark.</note> speak illogically. After he has said that *Christ*, having come to the fig tree, found nothing but leaves, he adds immediately

<summary>The author argues that Mark 11:13 is logically sound because Judea had multiple varieties of fig trees that ripened at different times, explaining why one tree might lack fruit while others were in season.</summary>
<keywords>Mark 11:13, Fig tree, Judea, agriculture, biblical interpretation, summer solstice, fruit seasons, Evangelist</keywords>

--- Page 454 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical analysis of Mark 11:13, specifically refuting alternative interpretations that claim it was the season for figs, which would make the text's statement that no fruit was found illogical.</meta>

...immediately after, *“For the time for figs was not yet”*; and this shows the reason why *Jesus* found no fruit there. But if you read it as those interpreters do—that he found nothing but leaves because it was the season for figs—what kind of logic is that? There were only leaves because it was the time for figs? There was no fruit because it was the season for fruit? Does such a reading make any sense at all?

Those who attempt to introduce that interpretation <note>original: "Exposition"</note> change only one word in the text—indeed, only a single-syllable word in the Greek original. Instead of <term>ou</term> <note>original Greek: "οὐ", a negative particle meaning "not"</note>, which is negative and results in the meaning that it was *not* the time for figs, they substitute <term>hou</term> <note>original Greek: "οὗ", which in this context would be used to change the sense to "where" or "since"</note>, which carries a completely opposite meaning. However, such a change goes against all the ancient copies and is without any justification—indeed, it is contrary to reason.

Some other commentators <note>original: "Expositors"</note> have tried to preserve the truth of the text by a method that is neither necessary nor reliable. Instead of "the time for figs," they read it as "the *year of figs*," which is a very forced interpretation and is offered without any evidence. What we have distinguished regarding the various <note>original: "divers"</note> kinds and the different seasons of that fruit is a certain fact, grounded in practical experience.

<vocab>Exposition, Greek Original, Interpreters, Expositors, Figs</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that attempting to alter the Greek text of Mark 11:13 to say it *was* the season for figs creates a logical contradiction and lacks manuscript evidence. He maintains that the existence of different fig varieties ripening at different times explains why Jesus expected fruit when the main season had not yet arrived.</summary>
<keywords>Mark 11:13, biblical criticism, Greek translation, fig trees, textual variants, hermeneutics</keywords>

## An Observation on the Cursing of the Fig Tree.

The Priesthood of *Aaron* and his successors, having been newly established, was further confirmed by the miracle of a dry rod, cut from an almond tree, which in the space of one night budded, blossomed, and yielded almonds (*Numbers* 17:8). This signified that the priesthood would flourish and bear fruit. 

But when that priesthood was about to expire, our Lord signified its end by a contrary miracle; for then he caused a tree, full of leaves, to dry up completely in the space of one night. This was to signify that the priesthood of the Old Testament—which was only a <term>type</term> <note>original: "Type"; in theology, a "type" is a person, thing, or event in the Old Testament that foreshadows something in the New Testament.</note> of the priesthood of *Christ*—would from then on be without fruit. Or else, it signified in general that the <term>Synagogue</term> <note>Referring here to the Jewish religious institution or establishment of that era.</note>, represented by the emblem of the fig tree, was near its destruction (*Luke* 13:6, etc.).

<summary>The author compares the miracle of Aaron’s budding rod to Jesus cursing the fig tree, arguing that the tree's sudden death symbolized the end of the Old Testament priesthood and the approaching destruction of the Synagogue.</summary>

### *A Digression concerning the Trees usually planted in the Land of Canaan.*

Their fruit was considered unclean for the first three years. in the fourth year, the *Israelites* offered them to God, though they were not permitted to eat them before the fifth year (*Leviticus* 19:23, 24, 25). However, this prohibition extended only to the fruits of the Land of *Canaan*. Now, why were the fruits of the Land of *Canaan*—the Holy Land—considered unclean, even during the first three years, while the fruits of other countries were never charged with any uncleanness? Would it not seem, to the contrary, that the holiness of the Land of *Canaan* should sanctify the fruits coming from her? Or at least, that they should not be considered unclean while the fruits of other countries were not treated that way?

This is full of mystery and relates more to the people than to the plants. That which is left as a matter of <term>indifference</term> <note>original: "indifferent"; in a theological context, this refers to actions that are neither commanded nor forbidden by God, and therefore neutral.</note> to those who are far from God is often considered an impurity in those who are near to him; for his presence obliges them to a more exact holiness (*Leviticus* 10:3). Similarly, many actions were lawful for the *Israelites*...

<summary>The author discusses the biblical law of Orlah, questioning why fruit from the "Holy Land" was considered unclean for three years while foreign fruit was not, concluding it symbolizes the higher standards of holiness expected from those close to God.</summary>

### Another Digression Concerning the Fruits Prohibited by the Law and Sanctified by Christ

The same law that pronounced the fruit of the first three years unclean declared that in the fourth year they should be sanctified by being offered to God. That law was fulfilled in *Christ*, who, in the fourth year of his ministry, having offered himself to God, has sanctified every kind of food, <term>abrogating</term> <note>original: "abrogating"; meaning to repeal or formally do away with a law or agreement.</note> all the laws to the contrary.

Indeed, were they not greater than the removing of the *Alps* or the *Pyrenees*? <note>original: "Pyreneans"; the mountain range forming a natural border between France and Spain.</note>

Such a miracle would be all the more remarkable <note>original: "considerable"</note> because it never happened. For a mountain was never removed from its place except by natural means—as, for example, by an earthquake, by gunpowder, or by the work of human hands—none of which is miraculous. The miracle would be if, without the help of such means but by a mere word or the mere sign of a man, a mountain should move from its place and relocate to another; but such a wonder has never happened. It is, therefore, proposed because of its rarity—indeed, as being something without example. And so it is with what the Apostle says: "*Though I have all faith, so that I can remove mountains*" (1 Corinthians 13:2).

### The time that passed from when the blood of Zechariah, the son of Barachiah, began to be avenged, until its full vengeance, Matthew 23:35.

The blood of that Prophet began to be avenged in **Joash** <note>original: "Joas"; the King of Judah who ordered the execution of the prophet Zechariah.</note> the murderer in the year of the world 3160 <note>This refers to *Anno Mundi*, a calendar system based on the biblical account of the creation of the world.</note>. It was then that he finished his reign, as his diseases made him unable to hold the scepter any longer; for that reason, the throne was placed into the hands of his son. Three years later, Joash was killed in his own bed (2 Chronicles 24:25). 

From that year, 3160, until **Christ** remembered the blood of **Zechariah** in the year 3960, it appears that exactly 800 years passed, which is twenty periods of forty years. From the time of Christ’s remembering that blood until it was fully avenged upon the **Jews**—which occurred at the final ruin of **Jerusalem**—there were forty years, making a total of 840 years. 

This total of 840 years equals 12 times 70. Thus, from the beginning of the vengeance for that murder committed in the First Temple to the completion <note>original: "consummation"</note> of that vengeance, which happened at the ruin of the Second Temple and with the desolation

### *If the ruin of the second Temple happened to avenge the blood of Zechariah, how can Christians say that it happened to avenge the blood of Christ?*

Those views are not inconsistent. If a murderer, who has received a pardon for his crime, commits another one, justice does not only punish him for the murder that followed the pardon. It also punishes him for the former crime, even though it was pardoned before; for falling back into crime cancels the cancellation <note>original: "abolisheth the abolition"</note> of his first offense. Therefore, two murders committed at different times and against different people are atoned for by one and the same punishment. Now, if this is considered just among men, should it be considered unjust

However, that desolation was only the beginning of the vengeance for **Christ’s** blood, which remains upon them and their children. This is proven by the scorn they face and the hatred all nations show toward them in the dispersion <note>the Diaspora, or the scattering of the Jewish people across the world</note> they have lived in for so many ages since. 

Therefore, what they have suffered since the ruin of the **Second Temple** is no longer for **Zechariah’s** blood <note>original: "Zecharias his blood"</note>, or the rest of the righteous slain under the **Old Testament**. Instead, it is for the blood of **Christ** and those **Martyrs** who have suffered for his sake.

<meta>This page continues the theological reflections on the events leading up to the destruction of Jerusalem and the significance of Jesus's final days in the Temple. It transitions from discussing the "blood of the prophets" to specific chronological observations of Passion Week.</meta>

### *The Last Time Christ Was in the Temple: An Observation on the Circumstances of That Day, Matthew 24.*

During the six days immediately preceding the burial of **Christ**, he dedicated three days to the **Temple**, honoring it with his presence, his teachings <note>original: "Doctrine"</note>, and his miracles. These were the first three days. On the last of these three, he left the Temple, never to return again, and foretold the complete destruction <note>original: "utter ruine"</note> of that building. Then, on the final day of the three remaining days, he tore the veil <note>original: "rent the vail"</note> of the Temple, which was a clear sign <note>original: "manifest fore-runner"</note> of the ruin he had predicted. The interval between these two events was only three days.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Jewish customs requiring ten men for specific religious and legal acts, linking these traditions to the Parable of the Ten Virgins.</meta>

...of ten men. They did not perform circumcisions, enter into marriage contracts, or issue a bill of divorce <note>original: "Bill of Divorce"; a legal document ending a marriage</note> except in the presence of that same number of men. Nor did they celebrate the Passover unless ten people partook of it together, although it was lawful for a greater number to be present. Even *Christ* conformed to this practice when he celebrated that sacrament. It seems that this Jewish ordinance was based on the example of *Boaz*, who, when he was about to marry *Ruth*, chose ten of the town's leading men to be witnesses (Ruth 4:2). It is also very likely that this is the origin of the custom of having at least ten virgins at a wedding ceremony <note>original: "Nuptial Solemnity"</note>.

The five wise virgins seem to call to mind the five daughters of *Zelophehad*, who were praised for their wisdom <note>original: "prudence"</note> in Numbers 27:1, etc. Often, the words of Scripture—beyond the subject they directly address—reflect upon other related <note>original: "collateral"</note> matters.

The Type and the Anti-Type <note>In theology, a "Type" is a person or event in the Old Testament that foreshadows a person or event in the New Testament, known as the "Anti-Type."</note> met within one and the same day: the Type or figure in the evening, and the Anti-type (the truth represented by the figure) in the morning. In this historical context, "Easter" was often used to refer to the Jewish Passover.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the institution of the Lord's Supper as a replacement for the Passover, specifically comparing different biblical "types" or symbols of Christ's sacrifice.</meta>

<page-num>154</page-num>

place of the Passover. We must here consider one particular detail in the variety of symbols <note>original: "Figures"</note> and sacraments ordained to represent *Christ* at his death.

That representation was first made by the slaughter of lambs and other victims used under the Old Testament. However, although they represented *Christ's* death, they did not properly represent his person.

During that time, *Christ* was also represented by a man, *Isaac*, who was offered in sacrifice and, in a sense, recovered as if he had been raised from the dead. But he did not actually die; therefore, he represented *Christ's* person rather than his death.

Finally, the bread in the Lord’s Supper and the breaking of it also resemble *Christ* and his death less than the other symbols that preceded that sacrament. For is not a broken piece of bread further from the resemblance of a slain human body than the bodies of sacrificed animals were? In those animal sacrifices, bloodshed was seen, yet it was not the blood of a human. In the case of *Isaac*, a man is seen being offered, but no blood is shed. And in the Lord’s Supper, we see neither a human body nor blood being shed.

Now, this we may say on that subject: before the death of *Christ*, it was more necessary <note>original: "needful"</note>

Indeed, the Lord’s Supper assumes <note>original: "suppose"</note> all those historical circumstances. That spiritual power residing in the body and blood of *Christ* is not represented by the shape <note>original: "figure"</note> or the color of the bread and wine, but by their nutritive ability <note>original: "faculty"</note>, in which they surpass all other food.

### Scripture never mentions the Soul of Christ except when speaking of his sufferings.

"My soul is exceedingly sorrowful." <note>Matthew 26:38</note> Also <note>original: "Item"</note>, "*My soul is troubled*" <note>John 12:27</note>. And, "*The Son of Man came to give his life as a ransom for many*." <note>Matthew 20:28</note> Indeed, in the Old Testament, *Isaiah 53* states: "*When you shall make his soul an offering for sin*."

Throughout the rest of all Scripture, the Soul of Christ is never mentioned except in the context of his sufferings. Although he did not always suffer in his Body, his Soul was nonetheless constantly sorrowful.

## Only twice did the Angels appear to Christ: The difference between those two appearances and an observation upon them.

It is true that the Angels have rendered many services to Christ since his coming in the flesh, but they appeared visibly to him only twice: once, after he was tempted in the Wilderness <note>Matthew 4:11</note>, and the other time, on the eve of his death <note>Luke 22:43</note>. The first time, many Angels came to him and ministered to him; but the second time, only one Angel appeared to him, and even then from afar—namely, from heaven. In former times, a whole army of Angels went to meet Jacob <note>Genesis 32:1–2</note>, but when Christ was in his Agony <term>Agony: This refers specifically to Christ's intense spiritual and physical struggle in the Garden of Gethsemane</term>, only one Angel appears to him, and even then does not come near him.

All of this has been a part of the humiliation <note>original: "abasing"</note> of Christ. The closer he came to the final degree of his humiliation, the more the Angels withdrew from him—with only one appearing to him then, and at such a great distance. Moreover, the fact that the Angel did not come out of heaven but appeared to him in glory, while Christ was

### *The angels never spoke in the presence of Jesus Christ: A review of the common opinion concerning the angel that appeared to him.*

**I**t is a common, though hasty, assumption <note>original: "rash prejudice"</note> that the angel spoke to *Christ* and offered him words of comfort. However, the biblical account <note>original: "History"</note> says no such thing; it states only that the angel appeared to *Christ*, strengthening him <note>Luke 22:43</note>. Some will ask, *How did he strengthen him, if not by speaking to him?* 

*Answer:* The simple, friendly appearance <term>apparition: a supernatural appearance or visible manifestation</term> of an angel was enough to show *Jesus Christ* that it came from God's love for him. That alone was sufficient to strengthen him, without the need for the angel to speak to him. In the same way, *Jesus Christ* himself appeared from heaven to *St. Stephen* and yet did not speak to him, encouraging him simply by his appearance <note>Acts 7:55</note>.

As for why they never spoke in his presence, much less to him directly, we may say that they showed by their silence that *Christ* received no instruction from them. In the wilderness, when they cared for <note>original: "ministered to"</note> him, we do not read that any of them spoke to him then. When they did speak, it was always in his absence. For instance, they spoke to the women who were looking for him in the grave, saying, "*He is not here*." They also spoke to the Apostles after *Christ* had ascended into heaven (*Acts 1:10*). 

As for why they never spoke in his presence—and much less to Jesus himself—we may say that they showed by this that *Christ* received no instruction of any kind from them. Several Prophets were informed by the angels appointed for that purpose. But as for the knowledge of *Jesus Christ*, he received it directly <note>original: "immediately"</note> from God. To make it clear that the angels contributed nothing to his knowledge, they always remained silent whenever he was present.

---

### Concerning the Twelve Legions of Angels (Matthew 26:53)

Why was the number twelve chosen? Although other interpreters offer various reasons for it, our own explanation is more natural, as it is drawn from the most important <note>original: "chiefest"</note> circumstance of the matter in question. The people who then had reason <note>original: "occasion"</note> to be afraid were twelve in number: specifically, *Jesus*

It then begins an analysis of why Jesus specifically mentioned twelve legions of angels during his arrest.</summary>

--- Page 474 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of why Jesus mentioned twelve legions of angels in Matthew 26:53. The previous page established that there were twelve people present who might have needed protection: Jesus himself and his eleven faithful Apostles.</meta>

*Christ* and the eleven Apostles who followed him; therefore, for as many men as were there, just as many legions of angels would have come to their assistance—one legion for each man, and even more, had *Jesus Christ* desired it of his Father.

A legion consisted of 6,000 men; consider what the proportion would have been of 6,000 angels to one man.

### Concerning the Rooster that Warned Saint Peter

Jesus Christ had said to him a little while before, and to the others as well, "*Watch and pray*." Now he gives him a warning by means of the rooster <note>original: "Cock"</note>, a watchful creature that makes itself heard to give warning to men. It marks the quarters of the night and of the day, and it alone of all birds wakes to crow at midnight.

God did not give the rooster a human voice. He never made any animal speak except to rebuke a sinner <note>original: "Reprobate"</note>; this occurred when a female donkey rebuked *Balaam* <note>Referring to the story in Numbers 22:28-30 where Balaam's donkey is given a voice to protest his treatment of her</note>. But such a miracle <note>original: "prodigy"</note> was never used for the conversion or the moral improvement of a man.

<summary>This page explains the significance of the "twelve legions" of angels as providing one legion for Jesus and each of his eleven Apostles. It also discusses why a rooster, rather than a speaking animal, was used to signal Peter's denial.</summary>
<keywords>Twelve Legions, Apostles, Matthew 26:53, Saint Peter, Rooster, Balaam's Ass, miracles, vigilance</keywords>

### Six High Priests whom the Scripture declares to have been guilty, and a seventh who has been more guilty than all the former.

The first six were:
1. **Aaron**, who, after the melting of the golden calf, murmured against **Moses**.
2. **Eli** <note>original: "Heli"</note>, whose indulgence toward his sons ruined both them and the people.
3. **Abiathar**, who had a hand in the conspiracy <note>original: "conjuration"</note> of **Adonijah**, **Solomon's** rival.
4. **Urijah**, who, to please King **Ahaz**, built an illegitimate altar <note>original: "bastard-Altar"</note> in the Temple.
5. **Seraiah** <note>original: "Serajah"</note>, who is included <note>original: "not accepted of the number," meaning he was not excluded</note> among the priests who persecuted **Jeremiah**.
6. **Joshua** <note>original: "Jehoshua"</note>, whose sons married foreign women during the **Babylonian** Captivity.

But the peak of all the crimes that defiled that holy Order occurred when the high "typical" priest <term>Typical: In theology, a "type" is a person or thing in the Old Testament that foreshadows or symbolizes someone in the New Testament (the "antitype"). Here, the author argues the Jewish High Priest was a symbol meant to point toward Christ.</term> condemned the true High Priest—namely, **Christ**. The priesthood which **Caiaphas** exercised ought to have confirmed the priesthood of **Christ**; instead, the symbol <note>original: "Figure"</note> has contradicted the Truth, and the shadow has attempted to destroy the reality <note>original: "the Body"; referring to Christ as the fulfillment of Old Testament "shadows" or symbols</note>.

<summary>The author lists six Old Testament High Priests who sinned or failed in their duties, arguing that their collective guilt is surpassed by Caiaphas, who used his symbolic office to condemn the very Messiah he was meant to represent.</summary>
<keywords>High Priests, Aaron, Eli, Abiathar, Urijah, Seraiah, Joshua, Caiaphas, Typology, Christ, Priesthood</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Christ’s trial and death, moving from the guilt of the High Priests to the role of the Jewish people and the release of Barabbas.</meta>

### Barabbas.

His name shows he was a *Jew*, and therefore it was all the more important for the *Jews* to have him punished so that they would not incur the suspicion of supporting <note>original: "abetting"</note> a seditious person; for they were very prone to rising up against the *Romans*, their superiors. By obtaining <note>original: "procuring"</note> life for such a man, they endangered their nation or exposed it to political <note>original: "State"</note> suspicions; but their madness corrupted <note>original: "depraved"</note> their judgment.

---

### Why did God pardon many of those who brought about Christ's death, but not Pilate's wife, who opposed that death?

God has set on the path of salvation many thousands of *Jews* who had crucified the Lord of glory (Acts 2:23, 37, etc.; 3:14, 15; and 4:4). The same history records <note>original: "rehearses"</note> that there were a great number of priests—whom we know were guiltier of the death of *Christ*—

--- Page 477 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding God's grace toward the Jewish people and priests who were involved in the crucifixion, contrasting their salvation with the situation of Pilate's wife.</meta>

who were since brought to the obedience of faith. Such were the effects of the goodness of God, who was willing to save them through the same blood which they had spilled, and cause their crime—the most heinous that could ever be committed—to turn toward their own salvation.

But if grace was shown to those people, to the murderers of the righteous one, to those who treated him as a criminal <note>original: "malefactor"</note>, why was it not also shown to that person who alone pleaded for him on this occasion and endeavored to deliver him? Among many answers that may be given to that question, it will suffice now to say that *Pilate's* wife was only moved in that matter by the fear of punishment, in case her husband should condemn an innocent man. Such terror is far from the true motives of charity <note>In this context, "charity" refers to the theological concept of selfless, divine love (Latin: *caritas*), rather than simply giving to the poor.</note> and the love of justice. Thus, among many persons who are guilty, God often pardons the guiltiest of all and leaves others to the condemnation they have deserved. In both cases, he shows that he has the power to show mercy or justice, regardless of the severity <note>original: "degrees"</note> of the offenses.

## The Death of Judas

**W**hen a man is so notoriously wicked that his evil surpasses all other men of his time, he usually becomes his own executioner <note>original: "Hangman"</note>. The Scripture mentions five men who were undoubtedly the most wicked men of their time, and all of them murdered themselves. One was **Abimelech**, the son of **Gideon**, who put seventy of his brothers to death. **Saul**, who was guilty of many notorious crimes. **Ahithophel**, a traitor and an abominable counselor. **Zimri**, one of the kings of Israel, who was extremely violent <note>original: "bloody"</note>. And finally **Judas**, whose crime surpassed all the crimes ever committed by men.

Let us add here an observation that is worth noting <note>original: "indifferent," here meaning a supplementary or neutral observation</note>. In Roman history, and in our own experience, there are more examples of women who have murdered themselves than of men; this has been noted by those who are observant <note>original: "curious"</note>. However, we must add to this that the opposite is found in the Scripture; for in all of sacred history, there is not a single example of a woman who ever killed herself. This is not because that sex passed so long a time without such tragedies occurring, but because

<summary>The author examines the biblical theme of notorious sinners, such as Judas and Saul, becoming their own executioners. He notes that while Roman history records many female suicides, the Bible contains only examples of men taking their own lives.</summary>
<keywords>Judas Iscariot, suicide, Abimelech, Saul, Ahithophel, Zimri, biblical history vs. Roman history, wickedness</keywords>

### Christ Praying for Those Who Crucified Him

Under the Mosaic Law, anyone who had accidentally killed someone was allowed to flee to one of the Cities of Refuge <note>original: "Towns of Refuge"</note> to escape the vengeance of the deceased person’s relatives. When the death of the High Priest occurred, that person who committed manslaughter was granted full freedom to return home and live there without fear of ever being harassed for that reason (*Numbers 35*). But Christ himself obtained a pardon for his murderers, praying to his Father to forgive them for that crime by citing their ignorance: *They know not what they do.*

A question may be raised here: the death of the High Priest freed the person who had committed manslaughter; but if someone had accidentally killed the High Priest himself, would that death have delivered the person responsible? Or would he have been forced to stay until the death of the next High Priest? Although this is not mentioned in the Law, it is more likely that the mistake which caused the death of a High Priest could not be wiped away <note>original: "blotted"</note>

<summary>The author compares the Old Testament "Cities of Refuge" and the High Priest's death to Christ’s prayer for his executioners. He then poses a legal question regarding whether the accidental death of a High Priest would grant the killer their own freedom.</summary>
<keywords>Cities of Refuge, High Priest, Jesus Christ, crucifixion, manslaughter, Numbers 35, Mosaic Law, forgiveness</keywords>

--- Page 480 ---
<meta>This page concludes the theological comparison between the Old Testament's legal Cities of Refuge and Christ's death. It then examines the specific language Christ used while suffering on the Cross.</meta>

...blotted out except by the death of another High Priest who succeeded the former one. But since *Christ* has no successor in his priesthood, those who murdered him could only find their atonement <note>original: "expiation"</note> through the very death they inflicted upon him.

---

### The Only Time *Christ*, When Speaking to *God*, Did Not Call Him *Father*

It happened upon the **Cross** when he cried out, "*My God, my God*." Until then, he had always called him "Father," even during his agony in the Garden and when he committed his spirit into God's hands. 

But that interval in which he cried out, "*My God*," is the center of his humiliation. During that time, he placed himself in the common rank of the believers under the **Old Testament**, who were not accustomed to calling God their "Father" when they prayed. Thus, he who alone had the privilege to call God "Father" refrained from doing so while he was in the depths of his abasement <note>This refers to Christ’s state of voluntary lowliness and suffering.</note>. We have provided several other reasons for this in our observations on the Creed <note>original: "Symbole," referring to the Apostles' Creed</note>.
